Commissioning brief: [`docs/provider-plugins-adversarial-review-brief.md`](provider-plugins-adversarial-review-brief.md)

**Note on the starter worktree.** The brief directs reviewers to a `module-saas-starter-six-integrations/` worktree as the merged reference. **No such directory exists in the workspace**; the six-integrations work was merged into `module-saas-starter` main as PR #70 (merge commit `8706324`, the current HEAD). Starter citations were therefore normalized to the `module-saas-starter/` prefix and spot-verified at that revision: `scripts/setup/sentry.sh:203`, `stripe.sh:196`, `stripe.sh:219`, `resend.sh:230`, and `provider-common.sh:110` all resolve to exactly the content the findings describe, and the two load-bearing credential-hygiene claims were confirmed in surrounding context (`sentry.sh:198-204` writes the *setup* `SENTRY_AUTH_TOKEN` into `error-tracking.secret.env`; `stripe.sh:213-222` writes the management `api_key` as the runtime `STRIPE_API_KEY` in `billing.secret.env`). The evidence base is intact; only the brief's path prefix was wrong. Update the brief's repository map accordingly.

Method: fourteen independent specialist reviewers covered the brief's 24 lanes and 40 adversarial scenarios against source and current official vendor documentation. Eighteen of the 51 `BLOCKER`/`HIGH` findings — every reported BLOCKER plus the first fourteen HIGHs — were then handed to separate agents instructed to refute them by re-deriving the evidence. Two were refuted and downgraded; three were confirmed on substance but adjudicated down in severity; the remainder were confirmed as reported. The verification pass is treated as the authority on severity, and its verdict appears on each finding it touched. Findings that fell outside the verification budget are labelled as unverified.

---

## 1. Executive verdict

### APPROVE WITH REQUIRED CHANGES

The lifecycle this proposal describes is the right one, and Codefly should build it. The phase machine (`Validate → Observe → Plan → Approve → Apply → Project → Doctor`), the stateless per-request `ProviderContext`, the environment-scoped binding, offline deterministic planning against a host-supplied observation, plan-digest-bound approval, ownership that refuses automatic adoption, retain-by-default deletion, and honest `UNCERTAIN` outcomes are all well chosen, and several are better than the precedents the brief asked us to compare against. The problem the proposal solves is also real, though it is mis-stated: what is actually broken in the current scripts is not port-copying but credential hygiene and the total absence of lifecycle state — `sentry.sh:203` writes a *setup* token into the runtime configuration, `stripe.sh:219` projects a management-capable key as the application's runtime key, and `stripe.sh:196` captures a webhook signing secret while persisting no record that the webhook was ever created. That justifies a first-class lifecycle on its own.

What must change before implementation begins is the security story, which is documented as reuse of existing enforcement and is in fact largely unbuilt or unenforceable. One finding survives verification at `BLOCKER`: **the one-time secret capture design fails on its own launch cohort.** Resend returns the webhook signing secret on plain `GET` retrieve, and Sentry's client-keys list response carries legacy `secret` and `dsn.secret` fields one JSON pointer away from the public DSN, so raw secrets reach the provider process during read-only `Observe` — which the mutation-framed capture mechanism does not cover at all — and when a response shape drifts, the declared JSON Pointer simply misses and the raw value is forwarded unredacted. This is the boundary that justifies the entire broker design, and it does not hold for two of the three launch vendors. Fixing it changes the manifest capture schema and the broker's response-forwarding semantics, both of which P1 freezes.

Three further findings were reported as BLOCKER, confirmed on substance, and adjudicated down to HIGH because their remedies are additive rather than redesigns; they are nonetheless the most serious factual corrections in the report. On macOS — the primary dogfood platform — the sandbox profile is `(allow default)` for reads (`core/runners/sandbox/sandboxexec.go:100-124`), so a provider can read the workspace, every projected `*.secret.env`, and all of `~/.codefly`; the one deny-list entry aimed at secrets points at a path nothing in the repo uses. Every agent is spawned with `cmd.Env = os.Environ()` (`core/agents/manager/loader.go:722`), handing the provider the operator's entire environment including 1Password session tokens and `CODEFLY_SCOPED_AUTHZ_SECRET`, which defeats credential minimization before any handle exists. And host-mediated egress to a manifest-allowlisted origin constrains credential *theft* but not data *exfiltration*, because for a third-party provider the allowlisted origin is the attacker's own vendor endpoint. The sandbox package's own documentation states it "is NOT a defense against a hostile binary that's actively trying to escape" (`core/runners/sandbox/doc.go:27-32`) — precisely the threat model the proposal assigns it.

Two structural corrections are also required, neither fatal. First, the permission stack the proposal proposes to "reuse end to end" is genuinely excellent inside `core/policy` but has **zero call sites in the CLI** that would run `codefly provider` — the CLI's own supervisor loads agents with `WithoutPrincipal()` and `WithoutSandbox()` (`cli/pkg/engine/supervisor.go:142-143`) — and three specific mechanisms the design depends on do not exist at any layer: host-side derivation of action and resource from the actual HTTP request (the permission callback currently trusts the plugin's claim, `core/policy/callback.go:286-298`), per-outbound-request authorization, and a human-CLI approval path. Second, the protocol cannot be frozen as written because it omits its own largest surface: the provider→host directions — broker request descriptors, checkpoints, output commits — have no proto, no version, and no negotiation rules, yet `Observe` and `Apply` are unimplementable without them.

The scoping question the proposal never asks is the one that should be settled first. Every provider named anywhere in the document is first-party `codefly.dev`-published, yet the entire untrusted-binary apparatus — new agent kind, sandbox, authenticated UDS, manifest ceiling, brokered egress, attenuated credential handles, declarative capture, conformance CI — is justified by "provider binaries may be compromised." The rejected-alternatives section never evaluates the obvious middle path of a trusted in-host engine with providers as reviewed first-party code plus a declarative descriptor tier for the long tail, which delivers the same lifecycle, state, digests, receipts, and projections at a fraction of the cost and can be promoted to the proposed architecture later by moving the same interface behind gRPC. That fork sizes all of P1 and belongs in P0.

**GitHub issue creation may proceed, but only after the seven P0 corrections in section 12.1 are made to the proposal.** Those are: decide the third-party-publication question; define origin admission for operator-supplied API bases (Sentry regional and self-hosted origins are a v1 input that the manifest-constant origin ceiling cannot express); resolve the three internal contradictions that each pin a contract P1 must freeze (the raw-secret manifest escape hatch, projection timing specified three incompatible ways, and the sink transaction contract versus one-time-capture durability); correct the reuse table so the broker, cassettes, CLI permission wiring, and provider conformance are labelled new work; extend the capture design to read paths with fail-closed matching; and restate the sandbox claims to what the OS backends actually enforce per platform. The recommended decomposition is **eight issues, not five** — the foundation must split so that the broker/credential/capture boundary is separately reviewable as a security artifact, and a Gate B "production observe ready" milestone must be added between local dogfood and production mutation. None of this invalidates the architecture; all of it is cheaper now than after `provider.proto` v0 ships.

### Convergent findings

These root causes were found independently by three or more reviewers working different lanes, which raises confidence that they are real rather than artifacts of one reviewer's framing:

| Root issue | Independently found by | Severity |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Raw secrets reach the provider on read paths; declarative capture is fail-open on shape variance | Lanes 8/9, Lane 14, Lane 15 | BLOCKER |
| Manifest-constant exact-origin ceiling cannot express operator-supplied API bases (Sentry regional/self-hosted, Stripe/Resend API base inputs) | Lanes 1/23, Lane 7, Lane 14 | HIGH, protocol-breaking |
| The `core/policy` stack is sound but has no CLI wiring; per-request action derivation, per-request authorization, and CLI approval do not exist | Lane 5, Lane 6, Lane 21 | HIGH |
| `toolbox-web` supplies almost none of the broker; the broker is new security-critical host code | Lane 2, Lane 7 | HIGH (one instance refuted as a doc error, one confirmed as substance) |
| Agent-kind routing is not one switch but ~20 inconsistent sites with three different silent fallbacks; the TOOLBOX kind is mis-routed in production today | Lane 2, Lane 3 | HIGH |
| Generic output contracts (`billing@1`, `email@1`, `error-tracking@1`) exist nowhere in code and no roadmap item creates them, yet manifests, bindings, projections, and conformance all reference them | Lane 11, Lane 22 | HIGH |
| `os.Environ()` inheritance defeats credential minimization for every agent kind | Lane 6, Lane 8, Lane 2 | BLOCKER |


## 2. Decision scorecard

Scores are 1 (unsound / must be redesigned) to 5 (sound and evidenced). Each score was assigned by the specialist who audited that dimension against source and vendor documentation.

| Dimension | Score | Evidence-based justification |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| Product value | 3 | The underlying problem is real and evidenced in the current scripts: management/setup credentials are projected into application runtime today (sentry.sh:203, stripe.sh:219, workos.sh:347-349), created remote resources leave no state (stripe.sh:196 discards the webhook endpoint ID), and there is no plan/drift/import story. But the proposal's headline value (founder UX, no copied ports, one command) is largely already delivered by provider-common.sh (endpoint resolution :104-114, hidden input, Git-ignore, 0600, doctor), while v1 keeps the dominant local frictions manual (vendor CLI forwarders and pasted signing secrets, provider-plugins.md:1370-1373, 1624-1629) and the funded roadmap migrates only 3 of 8 integrations, leaving a split script/provider system indefinitely. Value is real but concentrated in credential hygiene and the production path (P5), not where the proposal advertises it. |
| Abstraction fit | 3 | The right parts: environment-scoped bindings (environments already own ingress and secret backends, core/resources/environment.go:78,134), observe/managed/disabled modes in one protocol (Terraform data-source and Pulumi Read precedent, verified via official docs), typed leaf protocol following the Toolbox precedent (core/proto/codefly/services/toolbox/v0/toolbox.proto:33), and projection into generic contracts. The wrong parts: the untrusted-binary process model is sized for a third-party ecosystem the proposal never declares (all named providers are codefly.dev-published); the manifest-constant origin ceiling contradicts v1's own user-supplied API bases (provider-plugins.md:649 vs :1673/:2095); the header-injection credential model plus 'signatures stay in the provider' (:475-477 vs :1443-1444) is jointly unsatisfiable for signing-based APIs; and the lifecycle protocol is frozen against a managed inventory of exactly two singleton webhook endpoints (:1918), so IMPORT/REPLACE/UpgradeState semantics are stabilized on the weakest possible evidence. Lane 23 pressure shows four assumptions breaking at or immediately after v1. |
| Codefly reuse | 3 | The reuse story is real where it matters most for safety composition: the authenticated per-spawn UDS loader with sandbox/principal/production-admission options (core/agents/manager/loader.go:637-1072), manifest->sandbox translation incl. loopback (core/policy/permissions.go:48-207), structured failures (base/v0/failure.proto), read-only configuration+op:// secret resolution with redaction (core/configurations/secrets.go; core/resources/configuration.go:169-178), the Ed25519 receipt/journal libraries (core/executionreceipt; cli/pkg/executionjournal), and doctor's versioned JSON contract are all reusable essentially as advertised, and the proposal is honest that the projection writer, secret sink, and provider advertisement helper are new. The score is capped at 3 because four rows materially overstate: the HTTP broker is presented as an extraction of toolbox-web when it is a new host-side security-critical component (toolbox-web passes Authorization headers through, has no method/path policy, and sits on the wrong side of the trust boundary); cassette infrastructure does not exist in code at all; execution receipts have no CLI producer wiring outside the opt-in gateway daemon; and agent-kind routing is four inconsistent hardcoded switches plus an unverified, kind-blind GitHub auto-download rather than a single 'add routing' task. Additionally the macOS sandbox cannot enforce the claimed read confinement, so the reuse of the sandbox primitive does not deliver the guarantee the document attaches to it. |
| Permission model | 3 | The chosen primitives are the correct, high-quality substrate: an intersection (never union) decision model (proposal:322-334) matching CeilingPDP∩SaasPDP; principal non-impersonation proven end-to-end (callback.go:262-274, loader.go:836, TestE2E_Authorizer_PluginCannotImpersonate); scoped authorization that binds action/resource/audience/CatalogDigest/RequestDigest/TTL/MaxUses with fail-closed unknown-caveat handling (scoped_auth.go, scoped_auth_v2.go:311-374); SaasPDP fail-closed + deny-never-cached (pdp_saas.go); v2 Ed25519 already production-wired for Mind writes (cli/pkg/gateway/prepared_mutation.go). That earns a solid base. It is held to 3, not higher, because the proposal materially overstates reuse: (1) none of the stack is wired into the CLI host that would run `codefly provider ...` — the supervisor uses WithoutPrincipal/WithoutSandbox (supervisor.go:142-143) and no cli/ code builds a Principal or PDP, so the entire provider authorization host is NEW; (2) the permission callback trusts plugin-supplied action/resource, so per-plan-action authorization must be host-DERIVED (NEW, confused-deputy risk); (3) there is no per-outbound-HTTP-request authorization or revocation re-check primitive (GatewayEvaluator mints one token per RPC); (4) no PDP emits RequireApproval, so the human-CLI approval-binds-plan-digest guarantee is unimplemented; (5) production admission does not reject break-glass/PDP-off (loader.go:609-632). None of these is a fatal design error — the direction is right — but they are substantial, must-resolve gaps that the current text treats as done, so the model is 'approve with required changes', i.e. 3/5. |
| Process/network isolation | 2 | The design intent is coherent and the primitives partly exist, but the load-bearing claims are unenforced on at least one supported OS and unwired everywhere outside the toolbox path, so the proposal currently documents more isolation than Codefly can deliver. Evidence for the score: (a) macOS provides no filesystem read confinement at all - '(allow default)' with only file-write and four home directories denied (core/runners/sandbox/sandboxexec.go:100-124), with read paths documented as advisory (162-170) - so on the primary dogfood platform a provider reads the workspace, every projected *.secret.env and all of ~/.codefly, which the proposal itself designates as the provider state root; (b) the sandbox package explicitly disclaims the proposal's threat model ('NOT a defense against a hostile binary that's actively trying to escape', core/runners/sandbox/doc.go:27-32); (c) network capacity is self-declared by the untrusted manifest and applied verbatim (core/policy/permissions.go:174-206), and production admission accepts 'network: open' - proven by toolbox-web/manifest_test.go:13-17 admitting a network: open manifest - so invariant 11 has no enforcement point; (d) every provider inherits the operator's full environment (core/agents/manager/loader.go:722, no --clearenv), including CODEFLY_SCOPED_AUTHZ_SECRET, which falsifies secret minimization before the credential-handle design is even exercised; (e) Linux bwrap omits --unshare-pid/--unshare-ipc while mounting host /proc (bwrap.go:107-121), leaking same-UID process environments; (f) sandbox and production admission are wired only for toolboxes (core/services/agent.go:148, cli/pkg/engine/supervisor.go:142, core/sdk/sdk.go:168) and UDS is opt-in with one caller (loader.go:297,416; core/toolbox/session/session.go:276) although Linux --unshare-net makes TCP loopback unusable; (g) CI is ubuntu-latest only (.github/workflows/go.yml:23) and the darwin loopback e2e is darwin-tagged, so the macOS profile is never exercised in CI. It is not a 1 because real mechanisms exist and several boundaries genuinely hold: Linux read confinement, network isolation under --unshare-net, write denial on both OSes, sandbox inheritance by child processes, per-spawn 0700 UDS directories with crash sweeping, 0600 callback sockets with the spawn-time principal bound as the authoritative subject, and per-agent process groups. It is not a 3 because the two headline claims the proposal relies on - no workspace/secret reads and no unapproved egress - are respectively refuted on macOS and unenforceable against a hostile manifest, and both fixes are host-lifecycle changes that must land in P1 rather than P5. |
| Secret lifecycle | 3 | The architecture points the right way and parts are verified real: read-only 1Password resolver (core/configurations/secrets.go:137-144) with failure-output suppression, scoped-authorization fields sufficient for attenuated handles (core/policy/scoped_auth_v2.go:99-122), Ed25519 receipts (core/executionreceipt/receipt.go:76-100), an explicitly correct no-rollback rule for persisted one-time secrets, and honest UNCERTAIN semantics. But the load-bearing capture mechanism fails as specified on 2 of 3 launch vendors' read paths (Resend retrieve/list and Sentry client-keys return secrets where no plan/sink exists and fixed pointers cannot address arrays), has no fail-closed miss path, provider spawns inherit the full CLI environment (core/agents/manager/loader.go:722) contradicting the no-credential invariant, purpose separation collapses at ConfigurationValue (proto has only key/value/secret), secret minimization is contradicted by whole-configuration resolution, and the sink contract cannot recover orphaned production secrets. All are fixable within the proposed boundary, but the capture redesign and env scrubbing must land in P0/P1 before any implementation trusts the boundary. |
| Protocol coherence | 3 | The core shape is right and evidence-backed: stateless per-request ProviderContext (no Configure session) matches the working toolbox leaf precedent (core/proto/codefly/services/toolbox/v0/toolbox.proto:33-83; core/toolbox/launch/launch.go:99-173), Observe/Plan/Apply separation is cleanly implementable for all three initial providers, and UNCERTAIN/receipt/failure vocabularies map onto existing wired primitives (core/proto/codefly/execution/v1/execution.proto:22-23; base/v0/failure.proto:150-170). But the document is not freezable as written: the provider→host half of the protocol (broker request descriptors, checkpoints, output commits) — its most security-critical surface — is entirely undefined; the Apply/checkpoint transport is unspecified, making the checkpoint-before-next-mutation invariant unenforceable; the plan digest binds volatile observation fields, destabilizing every approval; output placement, state-version negotiation, downgrade behavior, pagination completeness, and RPC size bounds are unspecified; and the text disagrees with itself on whether the protocol is v0 or 1. All defects are fixable within the proposed architecture, which is why this is a 3 rather than a 2 — but v0 freeze must be blocked until the freeze-list items in the special artifact are specified. |
| State and recovery | 3 | The recovery philosophy is honest and the claimed journal reuse is real: cli/pkg/executionjournal enforces exactly the ADMITTED/STARTED/terminal ordering, attempt-binding immutability, and crash-sweep primitives (IncompleteAttempts, journal.go:432-470) the proposal needs, and 8 of the 11 required failure timelines resolve with UNCERTAIN + reconciliation once the sweep is wired. But the state layer itself has two structural defects — path-hash addressing with no symlink resolution, no stable workspace identity, and no relocation path (runtime.go:62-78 precedent, workspace.go:40-75), and per-binding stores that render the cross-binding ownership-uniqueness invariant (provider-plugins.md:1294-1295) unenforceable — plus four specification gaps: lock scope/CAS atomicity, dual-store (state vs journal) write ordering, state-schema downgrade behavior, and lost-response recovery for one-time-secret resources (no pre-send checkpoint, no idempotent-replay carve-out, no ownership stamping). None requires an RPC-surface redesign, but two (backend scope, apply checkpoint semantics) are breaking-if-deferred state/protocol contracts, which caps the score at 3. |
| Provider API fit | 4 | Stripe fits the proposed cross-provider contract unusually well and most load-bearing assumptions verify against current primary docs: full webhook-endpoint CRUD, secret 'Only returned at creation' (docs.stripe.com/api/webhook_endpoints/object), POST-wide idempotency keys with ≥24h retention and exact-body replay (docs.stripe.com/api/idempotent_requests), per-request Stripe-Version plus endpoint-creation api_version pinning (docs.stripe.com/api/versioning), metadata for ownership stamping, ≤16-endpoint bounded listing, and a CLI forwarding model matching local-forwarded exactly. Two verified misfits keep it from 5: the design's live-guard depends on observing account mode that GET /v1/account does not expose while the provider cannot see the key prefix (the enforcement mechanism is unspecified and must move to the host), and the plan model treats a pinned api_version change as an UPDATE with 'secret: unchanged' when Stripe makes it a REPLACE that mints a new signing secret. Both are correctable inside the existing action vocabulary and credential-handle model without protocol redesign, and the restricted-key story is qualifiable rather than broken, so the fit is strong but not yet accurate as written. |
| Local dogfood | 3 | The local path stands on real, verified machinery: deterministic port hashing gives stable callback origins across restarts (core/network/port.go:117-132), projections land in the exact files the loader already reads and the runner already injects (core/configurations/local_reader.go:64; cli/pkg/orchestration/runner.go:273) so no SDK bypass exists, plaintext local secrets are a sanctioned escape hatch (secrets.go warnPlaintext local exemption), and the scripts have already proven gitignore/0600/idempotency discipline that the writer can inherit (provider-common.sh). The design also correctly refuses hardcoded ports everywhere. But the dogfood loop's weakest links are exactly the parts v1 declines to own: forwarder companions are unsupervised session processes whose secrets go stale on every restart (Resend's temporary webhook is destroyed on exit per official CLI docs) with no doctor detection; Stripe local-forwarded mode depends on a manual terminal-to-prompt secret paste; the silent static-hash fallback can wire the forward target to a port nothing listens on when the flow daemon is down; projection changes are invisible to running services until an untracked restart; and CI dogfood without ingress rests entirely on hand-supervised vendor CLIs whose easiest auth path violates the no-secrets-in-argv goal. A founder's happy path works; the second-day paths (restart forwarder, restart flow, disconnect, CI) each have an unspecified failure mode. |
| Production readiness path | 2 | The proposal correctly refuses production mutation before remote state and a writable sink exist, and its Gate-C list (provider-plugins.md:2232-2240) is substantive. But it defines two gates where three operating states exist: 'read-only production providers may run' (provider-plugins.md:1010-1015) creates a production-observe state with no mandatory feature list — the exact vague-'ready' failure the brief rejects — and that state still spends live management credentials. Compounding this, none of the policy composition the gates depend on is wired anywhere today: WithPrincipal/WithScopedAuthSecret/WithPermissionsCallback/WithProductionAdmission exist in core (core/agents/manager/loader.go:485-603) with zero cli/ call sites, so every gate item is first-time integration presented under a 'reuse' framing. P5 additionally fuses Gate-C infrastructure with GA documentation into one milestone. |
| Testability | 4 | The five-tier architecture is genuinely strong: Plan is deliberately offline against supplied observations (provider-plugins.md:1056-1060) making planning testable without fake vendors; the permission negative matrix (provider-plugins.md:583-600) is an unusually concrete falsification checklist; replay-through-the-real-broker is the right 'no mocks' interpretation; Ed25519 receipts and the bbolt UNCERTAIN journal already exist to assert against (core/executionreceipt/receipt.go:26,85-131; cli/pkg/executionjournal/journal.go:432,796-808). Deductions: the two enabling infrastructures are misbudgeted — agent ci conformance is service-only and the provider mode is a new harness (cli/cmd/agents/ci.go:427-428, 554-579), and no cassette recorder/sanitizer code exists anywhere (doc-only, core/docs/testing.md:135-150); the sanitizer failure criterion, deterministic matching vs injected idempotency keys, the conformance provider's remote fixture, and the sandbox-assertion platform matrix are all unspecified. |
| Operability/UX | 3 | Strong foundation and honest intent: the doctor pattern the proposal extends genuinely exists, is qualified against a real provider, read-only, code-stable, and JSON-versioned (cli/cmd/doctor_workspace.go, cli/docs/doctor-workspace-qualification.md); the setup orchestration mirrors the proven script lifecycle; machine output and receipt correlation IDs are well conceived; the structured-failure envelope is real and reusable (core/proto/codefly/base/v0/failure.proto). But the operator contract is unfinished in ways automation will hit immediately: no exit codes, plan files that cannot travel between CI runners with no statement saying so, an approval flow with no defined grantor for the solo founder the product targets, a doctor extension that contradicts the existing command's documented no-agent/30s contract and would spend shared vendor rate budgets (Resend 10 rps/team), a diagnostic code list that cannot distinguish a Codefly policy denial from a bad vendor key, and 13 commands where 8 suffice. Nothing here requires redesign — hence 3, not 2 — but each gap lands on the first CI pipeline or first support ticket. |
| Migration | 4 | The safety property the brief demands — no migration path silently adopts or deletes a remote resource — holds by design: URL matches become unmanaged conflicts requiring exact-ID import (provider-plugins.md:1955-1962, 1286-1295), deletion defaults to retain, and the writer refuses unowned differing outputs; this directly corrects the current scripts' unsafe patterns (URL matching with no persisted ID, stripe.sh:168-176; management key projected as runtime, stripe.sh:222-226). Deductions: import-ID sourcing UX is unspecified for resources whose IDs were never recorded; ownership-metadata storage location is undefined during script coexistence; the shim is not behavior-preserving and per-flag semantics (--provision-webhook, --skip-remote-validation, --force) are undeclared; old CLIs silently drop provider-bindings (permissive yaml.v3, core/resources/marshalling.go:62-69) with no version signaling. |
| Roadmap executability | 3 | The dependency spine is correct and consciously designed: foundation first, Stripe freezes v0, Sentry/Resend explicitly parallel afterward (provider-plugins.md:2205-2207), hardening research overlapped — and the stable-only-after-all-three matrix (provider-plugins.md:1908-1931) reconciles the freeze with invariant 35. But the five-issue decomposition is not executable as written: issue 1 spans core+cli and eight deliverable families including the security-critical broker/capture surface, far beyond safe review; no owning repositories are named; the three new provider repos and the starter migration work are implicit; the output contract registry (billing@1/email@1/error-tracking@1) that must freeze before P2-P4 parallelism has no deliverable and exists nowhere in code (rg returns nothing); and live-acceptance prerequisites (dedicated accounts, Resend DNS-verified domain) are absent from the plan despite gating three exit gates. The replacement 8-issue map preserves the same spine while fixing reviewability and ownership. |

**Unweighted mean: 3.1 / 5.**

## 3. Blocking findings

1 finding survives the verification pass at the brief's `BLOCKER` bar. Four were reported as BLOCKER by the review panel; all four were confirmed on substance by independent verifiers, but three were adjudicated down to HIGH on the grounds that their fixes are additive (sandbox-profile hardening, spawn-environment allowlisting, manifest/descriptor scoping) rather than protocol or trust-model redesigns. Those three appear in section 4 with their original severity noted; they are not less real, only differently sequenced. The three downgraded items share one root cause — **the proposal's process-isolation claims are asserted against a sandbox whose own documentation disclaims that threat model** (`core/runners/sandbox/doc.go:27-32`).

### Confirmed blocker

#### ADV-001 — One-time secret capture / broker read path

- **Severity:** BLOCKER  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** yes  **Affected phase/issues:** P0/P1 (broker + manifest schema), gates P3 Sentry and P4 Resend acceptance
- **Verification:** CONFIRMED — Keep BLOCKER — the provider-process secret boundary that justifies the broker design fails on the basic Observe path for 2 of 3 launch vendors, and the fix requires redesigning the P1-frozen manifest capture schema and broker response-forwarding semantics before implementation starts.
  - Verifier reasoning: The finding survives adversarial re-derivation on all three axes. (1) Evidence holds: Resend's current docs state verbatim that create, retrieve, or list webhook calls return the signing secret in the response body (verified via WebFetch), and the proposal itself admits this at provider-plugins.md:81-87 and :224; Sentry's list-client-keys response includes `secret` and `dsn.secret` alongside the public DSN (verified via WebFetch). Both are launch vendors (:215-217). (2) The failure sequence is reachable exactly as described: Observe is read-only and runs before Plan (:1050, :1056), approval binds a plan digest (:553-555), a prepared output/secret transaction is an Apply precondition (:1173), and secret persistence is a high-risk action requiring approval (:402, :549) — so at Observe time no prepared, authorized sink exists and capture step 3 (:520-521) cannot fire. The capture mechanism is explicitly framed around successful mutation responses (:495, :525) with a single RFC 6901 JSON Pointer (example `/secret`, :511), which cannot wildcard across list-array elements. No other section strips secrets from read responses: the broker requirements mention only undefined "response body classification" (:1435), while :527 and :1443-1445 confirm the provider agent receives and parses response bodies. This directly falsifies the proposal's own stated boundary — "Letting the provider process receive the raw response would undo the credential-handle boundary" (:495-497) and "redacted from provider responses" (:488-491) — under its own threat model of a compromised provider binary (:1381). The Resend P4 scope mandates webhook observe/import (:2127), guaranteeing the path is exercised. Minor imprecision: brief invariant 18 (:509-510) literally enumerates state/logs/plans/diagnostics/receipts/cassettes/args/Git, not provider process memory; the falsified promise is the proposal's capture-section boundary and brief invariants 2/14 in substance. This nuance does not weaken the finding. (3) Severity: BLOCKER per brief:834 is justified — the capture declaration schema and broker forwarding semantics are P1-frozen protocol surface, and the required fixes (read-path captures, wildcard pointers, redact/suppress disposition, response re-serialization/stripping) are a breaking redesign of the trust boundary that is the broker's core justification, proven by primary vendor documentation (HIGH confidence) for 2 of 3 launch vendors.
  - Verifier evidence: core/docs/provider-plugins.md:81-87 (proposal admits Resend read paths return signing secret), :224, :488-491 and :495-497 (provider-process boundary promise), :505-514 (single-pointer POST capture example), :516-533 (capture requires already prepared/authorized sink; host prepares sink before the call; mutation framing), :1050 and :1056 (Observe read-only, pre-Plan), :1173 (prepared secret transaction is an Apply requirement), :402 and :549 (secret persistence is high-risk/approval-bound), :1435 and :1443-1445 (broker forwards bodies; provider parses responses), :2127 (Resend webhook observe/import required); provider-plugins-adversarial-review-brief.md:509-510 (invariant 18 text), :834 (BLOCKER definition); https://resend.com/docs/webhooks/verify-webhooks-requests ("Calls to create, retrieve, or list webhooks will also return the signing secret in the response body"); https://docs.sentry.io/api/projects/list-a-projects-client-keys/ (response includes `secret` and `dsn.secret`)
- **Claim challenged:** "The proposal does not allow raw secret-bearing responses to reach the provider process" and one-time capture (manifest-declared method/path/content-type/JSON Pointer into a prepared sink) is sufficient to enforce it.
- **Evidence:** Proposal 'One-time secret capture' section (core/docs/provider-plugins.md, secret-captures example with single pointer /secret; capture persists 'directly into the already prepared and authorized secret sink target'). Vendor read paths return secrets: Resend — 'Calls to create, retrieve, or list webhooks will also return the signing secret in the response body' (https://resend.com/docs/webhooks/verify-webhooks-requests); Sentry — client-key list response includes `secret` and `dsn.secret` alongside the public DSN (https://docs.sentry.io/api/projects/list-a-projects-client-keys/). Observe is defined as read-only with no approved plan and no prepared sink, so capture as specified cannot fire during Observe.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Provider runs Observe on a Resend binding: broker GETs /webhooks (list). Response body contains signing_secret for every webhook. No plan exists, no sink is prepared, no capture declaration can fire (and a fixed pointer cannot address array elements /data/*/signing_secret). Broker forwards the raw body to the provider process. A compromised provider now holds a live webhook signing secret despite every credential-handle control working as designed. Identical failure for Sentry client-key discovery, where legacy secret key material (`secret`, `dsn.secret`) reaches the provider during the flagship 'setup token discovers public DSN' flow.
- **Impact:** Intended invariant 18 and the capture mechanism's core promise are falsified by 2 of the 3 launch vendors' documented current behavior; the provider-process secret boundary — the main justification for the broker over raw egress — does not hold.
- **Required change:** Redesign capture before P1 freeze: (1) capture declarations apply to read operations too, with array-wildcard/pattern pointers; (2) add a sink-less 'suppress/redact' disposition for observe-time secrets (replace with presence/fingerprint, no persistence); (3) broker always re-serializes the parsed body and never forwards original raw bytes; (4) manifest declares all known secret-bearing fields per resource type and the broker strips them from every response regardless of operation.
- **Acceptance test:** Conformance fixture: replay a recorded Resend webhook list response and a Sentry client-keys response through the broker during Observe; assert byte-level that no whsec_/secret-DSN value crosses the UDS to the provider process, appears in observation output, state, cassettes, or diagnostics, while public DSN and webhook metadata still arrive.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 8, 9

## 4. High, medium, and low findings

48 HIGH, 57 MEDIUM, and 26 LOW findings follow. Findings that went through the verification pass carry its verdict and reasoning; that includes three HIGHs adjudicated down from BLOCKER and two findings adjudicated down from HIGH into the MEDIUM and LOW groups. Everything else was outside the verification budget and is reported as found — treat its confidence field as the finding reviewer's own assessment.

### HIGH (48)

#### ADV-002 — Lane 3 / agent-kind routing

- **Severity:** HIGH  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1 (foundation); blocks P2-P4 distribution
- **Verification:** CONFIRMED — Keep HIGH/HIGH — per the brief, HIGH means must be resolved before the affected phase (P1 foundation) is accepted with source-proven evidence, which this has; it is not a BLOCKER because it does not make the protocol/trust boundary unsafe or force a breaking redesign, only a P1 implementation-ordering requirement (centralize kind routing and fix the toolbox drift before registering PROVIDER).
  - Verifier reasoning: Every cited source location verified verbatim: build.go:514-519 defaults unknown kinds to agents/services (only application/module branched); versions.go:496-507 agentSubdir defaults to modules; manager.go:78-85 FindLocalLatest has no toolbox branch and defaults to modules; agent.go:160-177 Path() fails closed and routes toolbox to toolboxes/; agent_release.go:400-421 errors on any kind other than ''/codefly:service/codefly:module; ci.go:427-428 requires codefly:service; install.go:52, versions.go:112, and kinds/service.go:39 hardcode resources.ServiceAgent in ParseAgent. That is >=8 independent switches with three divergent silent fallbacks (services, modules, fail-closed error), exactly as claimed. The live drift proof also holds on this host: toolbox-web and toolbox-nix declare kind codefly:toolbox, their binaries (web__0.0.9-0.0.14, nix__0.0.8-0.0.13) sit under ~/.codefly/agents/services/codefly.dev, and ~/.codefly/agents/toolboxes does not exist — so the same toolbox agent resolves to three different directories depending on code path (build → services/, Path()/versions inventory → toolboxes/, FindLocalLatest → modules/), and manager.Load/Download resolve via Path() (loader.go:670, downloader.go:47/116/129), meaning a locally built toolbox is invisible to the canonical load path. Reachability: the failure sequence for PROVIDER is contingent on missing one switch, but the toolbox precedent — a kind whose introduction also intended full routing and still missed build.go and manager.go — demonstrates the piecemeal mechanism has already produced this exact failure once, and the silent defaults guarantee misses surface as wrong-directory resolution rather than errors. The proposal (reuse table rows 243-245, Agent information section, P1) enumerates the touchpoints (slightly more than the finding admits: path, local latest, build, list, versions, publish, CI, install) but nowhere mandates centralization, unknown-kind hard errors at the fallback sites, or fixing the existing toolbox drift; no other section prevents the failure. One caveat: the finding's phrasing that the proposal requires 'only' adding routing compresses the proposal's fuller enumeration, and 'cannot ship a working path' overstates impossibility — a complete implementation of the enumerated list would work — but the systemic-fragility claim, the required change, and the live prior-drift evidence all stand.
  - Verifier evidence: cli/cmd/agents/build.go:514-519; cli/cmd/agents/versions.go:496-507 and :112; core/agents/manager/manager.go:78-85; core/resources/agent.go:126-141,160-177; core/agents/manager/loader.go:670; core/agents/manager/downloader.go:47,116,129; cli/cmd/publish/agent_release.go:400-421; cli/cmd/agents/ci.go:421-428; cli/cmd/agents/install.go:52; cli/cmd/agents/kinds/service.go:39; toolbox-web/agent.codefly.yaml:2 (kind: codefly:toolbox); live host: ~/.codefly/agents/services/codefly.dev/web__0.0.9..0.0.14 and nix__0.0.8..0.0.13 present, ~/.codefly/agents/toolboxes absent; core/docs/provider-plugins.md:243-245 (reuse table), ~:1032-1036 (Agent information: 'provider routing in agent path, local latest resolution, build, list, versions, publish, and CI'), P1 section ('add provider agent install/build/list/version/publish routing'); no centralization/single-source-of-truth mandate anywhere in the proposal (rg -i 'centraliz|single source' returns no hits).
- **Claim challenged:** Proposal reuse table: agent identity/kinds reuse requires only 'add PROVIDER / codefly:provider routing' and 'include provider kind in path and inventory switches' (core/docs/provider-plugins.md, reuse table rows 1-3 and 'Agent information' section).
- **Evidence:** Kind routing is >= 8 independent switches with three divergent silent fallbacks: cli/cmd/agents/build.go:514-519 (subdir switch recognizes only application/module, everything else → 'services'); cli/cmd/agents/versions.go:496-507 (agentSubdir default → 'modules'); core/agents/manager/manager.go:78-85 (FindLocalLatest if/else default → 'modules'); core/resources/agent.go:160-177 (Path() fails closed); cli/cmd/publish/agent_release.go:400-421 (release semantics only for ''/codefly:service and codefly:module, error otherwise); cli/cmd/agents/ci.go:427-428 ('agent conformance currently requires kind codefly:service'); cli/cmd/agents/install.go:52 and versions.go:112 and kinds/service.go:39 (ParseAgent hardcodes resources.ServiceAgent). Live proof of drift for the LAST kind added: toolbox binaries web__*/nix__* are installed under ~/.codefly/agents/services/codefly.dev (build.go default), while Path() and versions.go route kind codefly:toolbox to agents/toolboxes/, and no toolboxes/ directory exists on this host.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** A provider agent built with `codefly agent build` installs under agents/services/, `codefly provider ...` resolving via Agent.Path() looks in agents/providers/ and fails with 'agent not found'; local-latest resolution scans agents/modules/ (manager.go:84) and silently resolves nothing or a wrong-kind binary; publish and ci reject the kind outright. Exactly this drift already happened to TOOLBOX.
- **Impact:** P1 foundation cannot ship a working provider install/build/resolve/publish/ci path by 'adding routing' piecemeal; silent wrong-directory fallbacks produce confusing failures or wrong-binary execution rather than errors.
- **Required change:** Before adding PROVIDER, centralize kind→{install subdir, GitHub repo prefix, release asset name, publish semantics, ci semantics} into the resources.RegisterAgent table (core/resources/agent.go:46-57) as the single source of truth, with unknown-kind = hard error everywhere (match Path()'s fail-closed behavior); then register PROVIDER once. Fix the existing TOOLBOX build/inventory mismatch in the same change.
- **Acceptance test:** Table-driven test iterating every registered AgentKind plus one unregistered kind asserting identical subdir/repo/asset answers from build, versions inventory, FindLocalLatest, Path(), publish, and ci — unregistered kind must error, never default; plus an end-to-end `codefly agent build` → manager.Load round-trip for a provider-kind fixture agent.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 3, 4

#### ADV-003 — Lane 3 / manifest packaging and ceiling enforceability

- **Severity:** HIGH  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** yes  **Affected phase/issues:** P1; precondition for P2-P4 installed-provider security and for P5 digest locks
- **Verification:** CONFIRMED — Keep HIGH/HIGH — must be fixed in P1 before the artifact/distribution format freezes (brief line 835), but the fix is additive format design, not a trust-architecture redesign, so BLOCKER would overstate it; every citation is directly source-proven, justifying HIGH confidence.
  - Verifier reasoning: Independently re-derived every citation. The proposal (provider-plugins.md:692-693, 744-746) asserts the manifest is "packaged with the release" and makes it the ceiling for the advertised catalog, while line 686 reuses "the same local, Nix, OCI, and GitHub resolution order." But all three release artifact formats carry only a bare binary: GitHub tar.gz extraction moves the single service-{name} binary (downloader.go:113-146), OCI layout is documented as a single binary layer (store.go:36-46), and Nix resolves to a bare file or bin/service-{name} (store.go resolveNixAgentBinary). Installed agents are single files name__version (agent.go:174-175; confirmed by ~/.codefly/agents/services/codefly.dev listing). The "mirrored" toolbox mechanism (toolbox.go:108-131 LoadToolboxFromDir) loads manifests from source directories only and has no release-artifact consumer, so it supplies no distribution mechanism. The reuse table (lines 244-245) scopes distribution extensions to kind routing only, and the P1 plan (line 2016) adds a manifest parser but never defines manifest-in-artifact packaging; a full-text search found no bundling/multi-layer/sidecar mechanism anywhere in the 2422-line proposal. The failure sequence is therefore reachable exactly as stated: a downloaded provider has no host-readable packaged manifest, forcing either deny-all (strict admission per line 349) or trusting the binary's self-advertised catalog, defeating the ceiling. Digest pinning is explicitly deferred to "a later agent lock file" (lines 687-688) and would cover only binary bytes under current formats. Minor caveat: the manifest-to-binary binding sub-claim overlaps with general supply-chain concerns, but the core claim (no manifest delivery mechanism at all for non-source installs) is fully proven.
  - Verifier evidence: core/docs/provider-plugins.md:686-693,744-746,244-245,349,1393-1397,2016; core/agents/manager/downloader.go:113-146 (extractTarGz + MoveFile of single binary); core/agents/manager/store.go:36-46 (single-layer OCI binary), resolveNixAgentBinary (bare bin/service-{name}); core/resources/toolbox.go:108-131 (LoadToolboxFromDir, source-dir only); core/resources/agent.go:174-175 and ~/.codefly/agents/services/codefly.dev listing (bare name__version files)
- **Claim challenged:** Proposal: 'Each provider source package includes a provider.codefly.yaml ... packaged with the release' and 'The running provider's advertised catalog must be a subset of the packaged manifest' (Provider manifest section); reuse table implies release machinery needs only kind routing.
- **Evidence:** No current artifact format carries a manifest: GitHub release assets are tar.gz containing the bare binary (core/agents/manager/downloader.go:59-70, extractTarGz:156, MoveFile); OCI artifacts are a single binary blob layer (store.go:36-46 'single layer: service-{name}'); Nix output is bin/service-{name} (store.go:434-438). Toolbox manifests load only from source directories via LoadToolboxFromDir(dir/toolbox.codefly.yaml) (core/resources/toolbox.go:108-131); installed agents on disk are bare files name__version with no adjacent manifest (verified: ~/.codefly/agents/services/codefly.dev listing).
- **Failure or attack sequence:** A provider installed from GitHub/OCI/Nix has no packaged manifest on the host; the host either cannot compute the manifest ceiling (deny-all, provider unusable) or falls back to trusting the binary's advertised catalog — which is exactly the compromised-binary self-attestation the ceiling exists to prevent. A tampered release can ship a binary whose 'packaged' manifest is whatever the attacker includes, with nothing binding manifest bytes to binary bytes.
- **Impact:** The manifest-ceiling security model — the proposal's load-bearing permission boundary — is unenforceable for any non-source-checkout provider. This is new artifact-format design, not routing, and changing the format later breaks distribution compatibility.
- **Required change:** Define in P1, as part of the frozen contract: the release artifact bundles provider.codefly.yaml with the binary (multi-file archive / second OCI layer / nix output file), the host installs and pins both, and the manifest is integrity-bound to the artifact (single artifact digest covering both, recorded at install). Local source-dir loading remains the dev path, mirroring LoadToolboxFromDir.
- **Acceptance test:** Install a provider from each store (GitHub tar.gz, OCI, Nix, local build); host loads the packaged manifest from the installed artifact without any source checkout, rejects a binary whose advertised catalog exceeds it, and rejects an artifact whose manifest bytes were modified post-publish (digest mismatch).
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 3, 4

#### ADV-004 — Lane 4 / missing broker & callback protocol definition

- **Severity:** HIGH  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** yes  **Affected phase/issues:** P1; blocks P2
- **Verification:** CONFIRMED — Keep HIGH/HIGH. Not BLOCKER: the fix is additive (define broker/capture-selection messages in the same versioned v0 package during P1 and include them in the freeze gate) and does not invalidate the trust-boundary design; per the brief's ladder it must be resolved before P1 is accepted, which HIGH expresses exactly.
  - Verifier reasoning: The core claim survives adversarial inspection. The proposal's only defined protocol surface is the 7 host-to-provider RPCs in codefly/services/provider/v0/provider.proto (lines 1022, 1044-1054), yet the architecture mandates a provider-to-host channel: 'Agent -->|request + credential handle| HTTP' (line 624), 'The provider should describe requests; the host should enforce transport policy' (line 1443), and 'The provider's request descriptor selects a subset' of manifest-declared secret captures (lines 500-503). No message schema, transport, version, or negotiation is defined for this channel anywhere in the 2,423-line document; the P1 core/proto deliverable (lines 2013-2014) enumerates 'information, validation, observation, plan, apply, doctor, state upgrade, diagnostics, output, and checkpoint messages' and omits any broker request/response, credential-handle-reference, capture-selection, or rate-limit-metadata message, while the broker itself appears only as a CLI/host implementation task (line 2028). The precedent citations verify: WithPermissionsCallback (core/agents/manager/loader.go:589) is the only existing plugin-to-host protocol and carries only permission decisions; toolbox-web performs HTTP inside its own process via its own httpClient with allowlist and CheckRedirect (toolbox-web/server.go:48,76) - the reverse trust direction - so there is nothing to reuse. The impact holds: authorization moment 3 (lines 442-445) must authorize 'the exact origin, method, path template, credential purpose, and idempotency identity' of a descriptor with no schema, and manifest method/path/JSON-Pointer ceilings (lines 505-514, 1434-1441) must syntactically match it. The freeze risk holds: line 2205 makes P3/P4 independent after 'issue 2 freezes protocol v0' and nothing places the broker descriptor inside that freeze, making it unversioned de facto ABI for every provider. Two corrections that do not overturn the finding: (a) P1 mandates implementing the broker, so P2 Stripe is not literally unimplementable - the defect is that the security-critical half of the protocol gets designed ad hoc outside the reviewable versioned contract and its freeze gate; (b) the required change overreaches on Checkpoint and OutputCommit - line 2014 already includes 'output, and checkpoint messages' in the provider v0 proto, and output commit is a host-internal act (moment 4, line 920) needing no host-side service. The load-bearing missing pieces are BrokerRequest/BrokerResponse, capture selection, and rate-limit normalization, which the finding correctly identifies.
  - Verifier evidence: core/docs/provider-plugins.md:624 (Agent -> HTTP broker edge), :1022+1044-1054 (only proto and its 7 RPCs), :500-503 (request descriptor selects captures), :1443 (provider describes requests), :442-445 (broker-execution authorization of undefined descriptor), :2013-2014 (P1 proto deliverables omit broker messages; include output/checkpoint), :2028 (broker is implementation-only task), :2205 (v0 freeze gate); core/agents/manager/loader.go:589 (WithPermissionsCallback, permissions-only reverse channel); toolbox-web/server.go:48,76 (in-process httpClient, reverse trust direction)
- **Claim challenged:** Proposal protocol section: the provider v0 surface is the 7 RPCs GetProviderInformation/Validate/Observe/Plan/Apply/Doctor/UpgradeState ('RPC surface' table), and 'The provider should describe requests; the host should enforce transport policy' (HTTP broker requirements).
- **Evidence:** The architecture diagram and text require a provider→host channel: 'Agent -->|request + credential handle| HTTP', one-time secret capture selected by 'the provider's request descriptor', checkpoints 'emitted immediately after each externally visible effect', and host-committed outputs. No proto, message schema, version, or negotiation is defined for any of these host-side services anywhere in provider-plugins.md; the only proposed proto file is codefly/services/provider/v0/provider.proto covering the host→provider direction. Existing precedent covers only the permissions callback (manager.WithPermissionsCallback, core/agents/manager/loader.go:589), not HTTP brokering — toolbox-web performs HTTP inside its own process, the reverse trust direction.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** P2 Stripe cannot implement Observe or Apply: there is no defined way for the provider process (sandboxed to loopback) to describe an HTTP request, reference a credential handle, select a declared secret capture, receive normalized rate-limit metadata, or report a checkpoint. Freezing 'provider v0' after Stripe would freeze a protocol missing its most security-critical half; a later broker-descriptor change breaks every provider.
- **Impact:** The manifest ceiling (method/path templates, secret-captures with JSON Pointers) must syntactically match a request-descriptor schema that does not exist; plan-action-to-HTTP-request authorization (per-operation authorization moment 3) is unspecifiable without it.
- **Required change:** Define in P1, in the same versioned proto package as provider.proto, the host-side services: BrokerRequest/BrokerResponse (origin ref, method, path template + params, headers subset, body, credential-handle ref, idempotency identity, plan-action ref, declared-capture selection), Checkpoint, and OutputCommit messages. Version them together with the provider protocol and include them in the v0 freeze gate.
- **Acceptance test:** Conformance provider performs a brokered Observe and a brokered mutation using only generated v0 broker messages; a request with an undeclared path template, caller Authorization header, or undeclared capture selection is rejected by the host with a typed failure; cassette recording operates on the descriptor schema.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 3, 4

#### ADV-005 — Lane 4 / Apply orchestration and checkpoint transport

- **Severity:** HIGH  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** yes  **Affected phase/issues:** P1 protocol shape; validated by P2 lost-response acceptance tests
- **Verification:** CONFIRMED — Keep HIGH. Per brief:834-835 this must be resolved before P1 freezes provider proto v0 (checkpoint messages are defined there), but it is not a BLOCKER: the trust boundary is sound, the durable plan/receipt/idempotency machinery bounds the crash ambiguity to a recoverable whole-apply UNCERTAIN state, and the fix is an additive protocol-shape decision inside P1 rather than a redesign.
  - Verifier reasoning: Every quoted phrase exists as claimed: 'execute one approved plan with checkpoints and idempotency identity' (provider-plugins.md:1052), provider must 'emit a checkpoint immediately after each externally visible effect' (:1182), broker 'checkpoints the persisted reference before allowing another mutation' (:525), 'Revocation is checked at every brokered sub-operation' (:561), per-request plan re-check at broker execution (:440-442), and invariant 25 'Every externally visible effect is checkpointed' (brief:519). The absence claim holds under independent search: no statement anywhere in the proposal fixes Apply as unary, server-streaming, or host-driven (rg for unary/stream/bidi/host-driven/per-action/iterate/progress yields nothing about Apply's shape); the P1 plan promises v0 proto 'checkpoint messages' (:2013-2014) with no transport, ordering, or durability semantics; the only checkpoint-before-next-mutation ordering rule (:525) is scoped exclusively to one-time secret capture; host receipts are defined at provider.apply granularity (ADMITTED/STARTED/terminal, :1187-1193), not per action; and the definition-of-done bullet 'every mutation is locked, admitted, checkpointed, and receipted' (:2222) names no mechanism. The failure sequence is reachable under a legal unary-Apply reading: :1182 says the provider 'emits' checkpoints but never says to where or when they become durable host-side, so a host crash after the broker forwards action 1 can leave no per-action checkpoint. Nothing else fully prevents it — the mitigations I found (durable plan digest with prospective resource IDs :419-420, idempotency identity :1179-1180, apply-level STARTED receipt backed by the existing fsync-backed journal whose Append commits before returning and whose IncompleteAttempts recovers started-not-terminal attempts, cli/pkg/executionjournal/journal.go:74-76,155-156,431, and the UNCERTAIN recovery flow :1213-1222 which already handles the harder dropped-connection case) bound the damage to a whole-apply UNCERTAIN state but do not deliver per-effect checkpointing, so invariant 25 is unenforceable as specified. One clause of the finding is overstated: the host is not fully unable to distinguish 'our uncertain create' from an unmanaged conflict, because the durable plan plus apply-level STARTED receipt plus idempotency identity provide attribution evidence at apply granularity; but that weakens only the impact prose, not the core defect. The brief itself corroborates that this is an intended-in-scope gap: Lane 4 directs reviewers to probe 'checkpoint semantics' and to 'identify decisions that must be frozen in v0' (brief:948-973), and Lane 10 lists 'crash after response but before checkpoint' as a required failure timeline (brief:1158). The required change (freeze host-driven per-action Apply or checkpoint-acked streaming in v0) is a genuine v0-freezing decision, exactly the class the brief demands be resolved before P1 accepts proto v0.
  - Verifier evidence: /Users/antoine/development/deus/codefly/core/docs/provider-plugins.md:1042-1075 (RPC surface table, no execution shape), :1052, :1165-1198 (Apply contract and provider.apply-level receipts), :1182, :1200-1226 (Partial and uncertain apply), :525 (ordering rule limited to secret capture), :561-565, :440-442, :2013-2014, :2222; /Users/antoine/development/deus/codefly/core/docs/provider-plugins-adversarial-review-brief.md:519 (invariant 25), :834-841 (severity ladder), :948-973 (Lane 4: checkpoint semantics, freeze-in-v0), :1154-1166 (crash-before-checkpoint timeline); /Users/antoine/development/deus/codefly/cli/pkg/executionjournal/journal.go:74-76,155-156,431 (existing durable pre-effect STARTED boundary the proposal does not wire per action)
- **Claim challenged:** Proposal: Apply 'execute[s] one approved plan with checkpoints'; the provider must 'emit a checkpoint immediately after each externally visible effect'; the host 'checkpoints the persisted reference before allowing another mutation'; 'Revocation is checked at every brokered sub-operation'; invariant 25 'Every externally visible effect is checkpointed'.
- **Evidence:** The proposal never states whether Apply is unary, server-streaming, or host-driven, nor how a checkpoint becomes durable host-side before the provider issues the next mutation. With a unary Apply, checkpoints arrive only in the final response, so a crash mid-Apply loses all intermediate effects, directly violating invariant 25 and the UNCERTAIN-recovery design ('Partial and uncertain apply' section). Per-action authorization ('Broker execution: before every outbound request, re-check that the action/resource is in the approved plan') already forces the host into the loop for every mutation via the (undefined) broker channel.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Stripe plan with two actions (create webhook, update events): provider creates the webhook via broker, host process crashes before the provider's Apply response; no durable checkpoint exists for the create; next plan sees an orphan remote endpoint and, because the create was never checkpointed, cannot distinguish 'our uncertain create' from an unmanaged conflict — exactly the ambiguity the checkpoint design exists to remove.
- **Impact:** Crash-consistency and revocation-between-actions invariants are unenforceable as specified; providers carry heavy orchestration (ordering, partial state assembly, compensation) that the host cannot audit, inflating each provider implementation and the conformance surface.
- **Required change:** Freeze the execution shape in v0: preferred — host-driven per-action Apply (host iterates the approved plan; provider executes exactly one plan action per RPC; host persists a checkpoint between actions and re-checks policy/revocation before each). Alternative — bidirectional streaming Apply where each mutation requires a host checkpoint-ack before the next broker request is admitted. Either way, 'Apply executes only planned actions' becomes host construction rather than provider promise.
- **Acceptance test:** Conformance test kills the host process between two plan actions; on restart, state contains a durable checkpoint for the completed action, the operation is UNCERTAIN, no automatic retry occurs, and the next plan converges via the checkpointed remote identity. A provider attempting a second mutation before checkpoint-ack is blocked by the host.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 3, 4

#### ADV-006 — Lane 4 / approval stability vs volatile observation digest

- **Severity:** HIGH  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** yes  **Affected phase/issues:** P1 message shape; P2 acceptance depends on it (no-diff rerun gate)
- **Verification:** CONFIRMED — Keep HIGH — fail-closed over-invalidation is an operability defect in the approve/apply loop, not an unsafe trust boundary (so not BLOCKER), but it must be resolved before the canonical-digest/plan phase is accepted, matching the brief's HIGH definition.
  - Verifier reasoning: All cited text exists verbatim and the failure sequence is reachable — in fact over-determined. The Observation section defines "canonical, bounded state" to include per-request volatile fields (safe response/request IDs, rate-limit status and retry-after); the Plan hashing list binds an "observed-state digest" that is never defined anywhere in the document (the term appears exactly once), so nothing scopes it to material state; "Changing any of these invalidates approval" follows immediately. Apply requires non-expired observation, the scoped-auth TTL is explicitly "short enough that a delayed Apply must be re-planned", and the CI plan-file workflow (plan --out / apply --plan, "primitive commands remain available for CI") exists as claimed. The digest-canonicalization function is deferred to implementation ("define canonical serialization/digest functions") with no volatile-field exclusion; the only material/advisory split in the doc is in the state-file contents list (drift fields listed separately from request IDs), showing the distinction was made for storage but not for the digest. Crucially, no other section prevents the loop — the opposite: lines 555-557 make invalidation categorical ("refreshing observation ... invalidates approval"), so even with a stable digest, no refresh can preserve approval; every refresh forces re-plan and re-approve, and interactive apply works only within the TTL window. The proposal also contains internal tension: Tier-2 conformance step 10 requires "rerun plan and require no diff", which cannot hold at digest level if volatile fields feed the hash. One fix-scope addendum: the required change must also amend the categorical refresh-invalidates-approval rule at lines 555-557, or splitting the digest alone will not restore refresh-preserves-approval behavior.
  - Verifier evidence: core/docs/provider-plugins.md:1108-1109 (request IDs, rate-limit/retry-after in canonical observation); :1148-1161 (plan hash includes observed-state digest; "Changing any of these invalidates approval"); :1172 (apply requires non-expired observation); :455 (TTL forces re-plan on delay); :555-557 ("refreshing observation ... invalidates approval" — categorical); :1478-1479,1499 (plan --out / apply --plan for CI); :1410-1411 (freshness control); :2018 (digest functions undefined, deferred); :1265-1269 (state file separates drift fields from request IDs — split exists for state, not digest); :1875 (conformance "rerun plan and require no diff" tension); brief :834-835,841 (severity/confidence definitions)
- **Claim challenged:** Proposal: the plan digest binds the 'observed-state digest', and 'Changing any of these invalidates approval'; simultaneously canonical observation must include 'rate-limit status and retry-after', 'safe response/request IDs', and last-observation metadata ('Observation' section), and 'Apply requires non-expired observation' with a scoped-auth 'TTL short enough that a delayed Apply must be re-planned'.
- **Evidence:** Both requirements appear verbatim in provider-plugins.md ('Plan' hashing list; 'Observation' bullet list; 'Per-operation authorization' TTL bullet). No text separates digested material state from volatile advisory metadata.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Operator plans, approval is granted; observation expires or is refreshed before Apply (per the freshness rule); the refresh returns identical remote resources but different request IDs / rate-limit counters; the observed-state digest changes; approval is invalidated; re-plan produces a new digest; repeat. Interactive approve-then-apply works only by racing the TTL; CI plan-file workflows (`codefly provider plan --out PLAN` then `apply --plan PLAN`) become systematically stale.
- **Impact:** Operability failure of the core approve/apply loop and of plan-file portability; predictable pressure to lengthen TTLs or weaken freshness checks, eroding the TOCTOU protection the digest exists to provide.
- **Required change:** Split the Observe response in v0 into (a) canonical material state — resource identities, provider-owned drift fields, account identity/mode — which alone feeds the observed-state digest, and (b) advisory metadata (request IDs, rate-limit status, timing) excluded from all digests. Define the canonicalization to be re-observation-stable when remote state is unchanged, and make 'refresh with unchanged material state preserves approval' an explicit conformance requirement.
- **Acceptance test:** Observe twice against a replay cassette with differing request IDs and rate-limit headers but identical resources: digests must be byte-identical and a previously approved plan must remain valid; changing one material field (e.g., webhook URL) must invalidate it.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 3, 4

#### ADV-007 — Lane 11 / configuration classification (scenario 18)

- **Severity:** HIGH  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1 (writer), P3 (Sentry example/acceptance)
- **Verification:** not independently verified (outside the verification budget)
- **Claim challenged:** Proposal's Sentry projection example: SENTRY_DSN, SENTRY_ENVIRONMENT, SENTRY_ORG, SENTRY_PROJECT are written to `error-tracking.secret.env or error-tracking.secret.ref.env`, and 'SECRET maps to secret=true' suffices for output classification.
- **Evidence:** Proposal core/docs/provider-plugins.md ('Example Sentry projection' block, lines ~947-961). Sentry official docs: 'DSNs are safe to keep public because they only allow submission of new events' (https://docs.sentry.io/concepts/key-terms/dsn-explainer/). core/configurations/secrets.go:66-78 (validateReferenceOnlySecret rejects plaintext: 'plaintext is not allowed; use a supported provider reference') enforced at core/configurations/local_reader.go:377-385. core/proto/codefly/base/v0/configuration.proto:8-15 (only a bool secret flag exists). core/resources/configuration.go:178-186 (IsSensitiveKey matches substring 'DSN'). core/agents/services/base_builder.go:651-653 and 868-880 (restricted deployment rendering hard-fails on any value with Secret=true OR IsSensitiveKey). Starter reality check: the public browser DSN already lives in the PUBLIC file (module-saas-starter/configurations/local-dogfood/error-tracking.env.example line 4, NEXT_PUBLIC_SENTRY_DSN).
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Over-classification path: host projects the backend DSN as secret=true into a reference-only sink (`.secret.ref.env`) — the loader rejects the plaintext DSN literal and the whole `error-tracking` configuration fails to load, taking down every consumer; with a plain `.secret.env` sink, restricted deployment rendering later hard-fails on the DSN-suffixed key. Under-classification path: a genuinely secret value (SENTRY_AUTH_TOKEN) written to the public env file is committed to Git (gitignore verification is only applied to secret targets — provider-common.sh:158-159 pattern the writer inherits) and injected into every consumer; IsSensitiveKey only redacts logs, it does not block the write or the commit.
- **Impact:** A correct provider release can render error tracking unloadable or leak a build token into Git depending on one classification bit, and the proto layer cannot even represent the four-way classification the protocol defines.
- **Required change:** Fix the projection example (DSN/org/project/environment belong in the public file, matching the starter); define the writer's sink-selection rule per classification AND per sink type (reference-only sinks may only receive references, never captured plaintext); add a writer invariant that public outputs are scanned with IsSensitiveKey-class heuristics and secret-shaped public values fail the projection; specify how PUBLIC/SENSITIVE/SECRET/browser-exposable survives into ConfigurationValue or its successor.
- **Acceptance test:** Tier-0 writer test: projecting a browser-exposable public DSN through (a) plain sink and (b) reference-only sink yields a loadable configuration with the DSN in the public file in both cases; projecting a SECRET-classified value whose target file is not Git-ignored fails before any file is written; a PUBLIC-classified value whose key matches the sensitive-key heuristic requires an explicit override recorded in the plan.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 11, 12

#### ADV-008 — Lane 11 / purpose separation vs consumer granularity

- **Severity:** HIGH  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** yes  **Affected phase/issues:** P1 (contract shape), P3 (Sentry exit gate)
- **Verification:** not independently verified (outside the verification budget)
- **Claim challenged:** 'The Sentry setup credential is not automatically copied into SENTRY_AUTH_TOKEN. Setup and build are different purposes' and 'build token is projected only to the build/release configuration that needs it' — while the projection example places SENTRY_AUTH_TOKEN inside the `error-tracking` configuration.
- **Evidence:** Proposal projection example puts SENTRY_AUTH_TOKEN in error-tracking.secret.env (core/docs/provider-plugins.md ~line 961) and Sentry credential-separation section (~lines 1704-1709). Consumer granularity is the whole configuration name: core/resources/service.go:57 (WorkspaceConfigurationDependencies []string); the starter frontend runtime service depends on `error-tracking` (module-saas-starter/module/services/frontend/service.codefly.yaml:14-18); cli/pkg/orchestration/runner.go:273 fetches the full named configuration (public + secret values) and it is injected into the consuming service's environment via core/resources/environment_variables_manager.go:279-296, 489-513. No key-level or purpose-level consumer scoping exists anywhere in core or cli.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Provider projects error-tracking@1 including the optional build token. The frontend service (a runtime consumer) declares `error-tracking` and receives SENTRY_AUTH_TOKEN as CODEFLY__WORKSPACE_SECRET_CONFIGURATION__ERROR_TRACKING__SENTRY_AUTH_TOKEN in its live process environment. The BUILD-purpose credential is now in a runtime process, directly violating invariant 16 ('a setup credential is not automatically projected into runtime') and the proposal's own Sentry exit gate ('setup token is absent from runtime output by default').
- **Impact:** Purpose separation — a load-bearing security property of the credential model — is advisory, not enforceable, because the projection contract has no unit smaller than a configuration name and consumers cannot express purpose.
- **Required change:** Before P1 freeze, make purpose→configuration-name mapping part of the contract definition: BUILD-purpose outputs must project into a distinct configuration name (e.g. `error-tracking-build`) that runtime services do not declare, or the binding must declare per-consumer output routing. The output-contract schema must forbid mixing RUNTIME/BROWSER and BUILD purposes in one configuration name. Fix the projection example accordingly.
- **Acceptance test:** Conformance test: a provider declaring a BUILD-purpose output in the same contract configuration as RUNTIME outputs fails manifest validation; starter dogfood asserts `env | grep SENTRY_AUTH_TOKEN` is empty inside the running frontend and accounts processes while the build/release step receives it.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 11, 12

#### ADV-009 — Lane 11 / contract versioning

- **Severity:** HIGH  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** yes  **Affected phase/issues:** P1, P2
- **Verification:** not independently verified (outside the verification budget)
- **Claim challenged:** `output-contract: codefly.dev/configuration/billing@1` provides versioned schema identity with 'compatibility/version negotiation' for projections.
- **Evidence:** ConfigurationInformation carries only a name (core/proto/codefly/base/v0/configuration.proto:27-35); consumers bind by bare string name with no version (core/resources/service.go:57; module-saas-starter/module/services/frontend/service.codefly.yaml:14-18). Writer invariant 11 'validate the resulting configuration through the normal loader' validates only file syntax, duplicate/conflict rules, and reference-only constraints (core/configurations/local_reader.go:307-348, 474-509) — there is no schema to validate against. The proposal never states where the billing@1/email@1/error-tracking@1 schemas live, which repository owns them, or how a provider's declared projection (`projections: contract: codefly.dev/configuration/billing, version: 1` in provider.codefly.yaml) is checked against actual emitted keys.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** provider-stripe 0.2.0 declares billing@2 and renames STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET. The binding's output-contract string is updated, plan approves, projection succeeds, loader loads fine — and the accounts service, which binds only the name `billing`, silently loses webhook verification at the next restart. No layer could have detected the incompatibility because no artifact defines what billing@1 contains and no consumer states a version.
- **Impact:** The entire product boundary ('application services consume generic contracts') rests on contracts that exist only as strings; cross-version breakage is undetectable, and the contract ID is baked into manifests and plan digests, so retrofitting changes the protocol.
- **Required change:** Before P1 freeze: define contract schema artifacts (key list, classification, purpose, browser-exposure, required/optional) owned in core, versioned with the contract ID; the host validates every projection against the declared contract version at plan time and at write time; decide and document whether consumers ever declare a version (recommendation: keys are append-only within a major version, and the doctor flags consumers when the projected major version changes).
- **Acceptance test:** Tier-0: a provider output containing a key absent from the declared contract version fails Plan with provider.schema.incompatible; a projection omitting a required contract key fails; bumping the contract major version with unchanged consumers produces a doctor warning naming each consuming service.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 11, 12

#### ADV-010 — Lane 12 / public ingress resolution (scenario 31)

- **Severity:** HIGH  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1, P2
- **Verification:** not independently verified (outside the verification budget)
- **Claim challenged:** Reuse table: 'environment ingress — Environment.Ingress public host mapping — resolve a public HTTPS origin for remote webhooks' presents public-origin resolution as an extension of existing behavior.
- **Evidence:** Environment.Ingress is a YAML data structure (core/resources/environment.go:79-84, field at :134) whose only consumers are a deep-copy in cli/pkg/orchestration/environment.go:44-47 and the gitops k8s bundle (cli/pkg/gitops/module_bundle.go:142,166) for kustomize overlay rendering. No function anywhere in core or cli resolves (environment, service, endpoint) to a public HTTPS origin. The current scripts confirm this: setup_public_origin returns the loopback address from `codefly endpoint` (module-saas-starter/scripts/setup/provider-common.sh:104-114) and remote webhook provisioning requires the OPERATOR to hand-supply `--webhook-origin` and pass a bare HTTPS-shape regex (provider-common.sh:123-127, stripe.sh:38). Stripe requires 'Registered webhook endpoints must be publicly accessible HTTPS URLs' with TLS 1.2/1.3 (https://docs.stripe.com/webhooks).
