- **Required change:** Add an explicit foundation deliverable: versioned generic configuration contract schemas (billing@1, email@1, error-tracking@1) owned by core (or an explicitly decided owner), machine-validated by the projection writer and Tier-2 conformance, with the starter's existing env keys as the v1 baseline plus documented additions. Freeze them at the same time as provider.proto v0.
- **Acceptance test:** The host rejects a provider projection whose keys/classifications do not validate against the declared contract schema version, and the starter's accounts/frontend services consume a billing@1 projection with zero code changes in the Stripe dogfood run.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 19, 20, 21, 22

### MEDIUM (57)

#### ADV-050 — Lane 3 / distribution naming hardcodes 'service-'

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1; blocks P2 publish/install
- **Claim challenged:** Proposal: 'The same local, Nix, OCI, and GitHub resolution order applies' to provider agents and repos will be named provider-stripe (P2 'create provider-stripe').
- **Evidence:** GitHub repo derived as service-<name> (core/agents/manager/downloader.go:27-32 toGithubSource); release asset name service-%s_%s_%s_%s.tar.gz (core/agents/manager/downloader_url.go:14-26); OCI layer titled service-{name} (store.go:40-46); Nix convention bin/service-{name} (store.go:330-334, 438); OCI repo path agents/{publisher}/{name} contains no kind segment (store.go:85-87), so a provider named 'stripe' and any service named 'stripe' share one OCI repository and tag namespace.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Version resolution and download for codefly.dev/stripe (provider) queries github.com/codefly-dev/service-stripe and asset service-stripe_*.tar.gz — wrong repo or 404; publishing a provider to OCI can silently shadow or be shadowed by a same-named service kind.
- **Impact:** Providers are undistributable through three of four stores without touching every naming site; cross-kind OCI collision is a supply-chain confusion risk for a credential-handling plugin class.
- **Required change:** Make repo prefix, asset prefix, OCI repo path (add kind segment, e.g. agents/{kind}/{publisher}/{name}), and Nix bin name kind-derived from the centralized registry; enumerate all five sites in the P1 issue.
- **Acceptance test:** Publish and resolve a provider-kind fixture through GitHub naming, OCI, and Nix; verify a same-named service and provider coexist in one OCI registry without collision and each resolves its own binary.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 3, 4

#### ADV-051 — Lane 3 / artifact integrity before P5

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1 decision; enforced before P2 dogfood
- **Claim challenged:** Proposal: 'Production bindings must pin a concrete semantic version. A later agent lock file should also pin the artifact digest' — digest locking deferred to P5, while P2-P4 dogfood runs providers against real sandbox accounts with real credentials.
- **Evidence:** GitHub download path has zero integrity verification: Download is http.Get + extractTarGz with no checksum (core/agents/manager/downloader.go:59-70, 153-226; ValidURL:34-43 checks only host==github.com); loader has no digest tracking (no 'digest' hits in loader.go). Only the OCI path verifies the blob sha256 against its manifest (store.go:157-189), and OCI tags themselves are mutable. GitHub release tags/assets are re-taggable, so a semver pin does not pin bytes.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** A re-tagged or MITM'd (repo-compromise) release asset replaces provider-stripe 0.1.0 with a different binary; the host loads it, mints credential handles for it, and its manifest ceiling (see manifest-packaging finding) is whatever it ships.
- **Impact:** Supply-chain gap for the highest-privilege plugin class during the entire P2-P4 dogfood window, not just production.
- **Required change:** Either pull digest locking forward into P1 (record artifact digest at install; verify on every load) or restrict provider-kind agents to digest-verifying sources (local build, OCI, Nix) until the lock file exists; state the choice in the P0 exit gate ('decide artifact digest lock timing').
- **Acceptance test:** Modify an installed provider binary (or serve a different asset under the same tag); next `codefly provider` invocation refuses to load it with a digest-mismatch diagnostic.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 3, 4

#### ADV-052 — Lane 4 / output placement and observe-mode lifecycle

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** yes  **Affected phase/issues:** P1 proto; P3 Sentry depends on it
- **Claim challenged:** Proposal: 'The provider returns typed output values to the host. It never writes files' (Configuration projections) and management mode `observe` 'projects configuration' — but PROJECT_OUTPUT is a plan action and only Apply consumes plans.
- **Evidence:** No RPC in the 7-row surface is named as carrying final typed output values; Plan carries only 'expected configuration projection changes'; one-time secret handles exist only during Apply (broker capture); the `observe` mode section says it projects configuration while `Apply | brokered | yes` is labeled as the mutating phase, and nothing states that observe-mode bindings execute a projection-only Apply. Lane 4 of the brief explicitly asks 'whether outputs belong in Plan, Apply, Observe, or a separate projection call'.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Two conforming implementations disagree: one returns outputs from Observe (Sentry-style, no Apply ever runs for observe bindings), another only from Apply; the host's output-digest CAS, receipts ('provider.apply ADMITTED/STARTED/...'), and provider.project.* authorization moments then fire on different paths for different providers, breaking the no-provider-specific-branch coordinator claim.
- **Impact:** Cross-provider contract matrix row 'projection-only/no mutation: yes/yes/yes' is untestable without pinning where outputs travel; Sentry (P3) is the first casualty.
- **Required change:** Specify in v0: final typed outputs are results of executing PROJECT_OUTPUT plan actions during Apply (which may be a purely local, non-remote-mutating Apply for observe-mode bindings); Plan carries expected changes only; Observe never carries outputs. Document that observe-mode bindings still traverse Plan→Approve(low-risk)→Apply for projection commits and receive provider.apply receipts.
- **Acceptance test:** Conformance run of a projection-only provider: outputs commit exclusively via an Apply of PROJECT_OUTPUT actions, produce output-digest CAS and receipts, and a second plan is empty; an Observe response containing output values fails conformance.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 3, 4

#### ADV-053 — Lane 4 / state versioning, downgrade, unknown-field survival

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** yes  **Affected phase/issues:** P1; exercised by P5 compatibility tests but frozen earlier
- **Claim challenged:** Proposal: GetProviderInformation returns 'state schema version' (singular); UpgradeState runs 'for every required schema step', is 'backed up before commit'; state contents are an enumerated host-defined list ('Provider state may contain...').
- **Evidence:** No minimum-upgradable-from version is advertised; no rule for an older agent encountering newer state (brief scenario 26) appears anywhere; no rollback semantics beyond pre-commit backup; the enumerated state-contents list leaves no defined location for provider-specific fields the host does not model, so nothing specifies that host operations (locking, checkpoint writes, receipts correlation) preserve provider-opaque bytes across host versions.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Agent 0.3 (state v3) writes state; user pins back to agent 0.2 (state v2) after a regression — proposal gives no defined outcome: silent misread of v3 state by v2 code corrupts ownership/remote-ID records, the exact data deletion-safety depends on. Separately, a host upgrade that round-trips state through its typed model drops provider fields it does not know, silently discarding drift baselines.
- **Impact:** State is the recovery-of-record for remote resources and secret references; undefined downgrade/unknown-field behavior converts routine version churn into orphaned or wrongly-deleted remote resources.
- **Required change:** In v0: GetProviderInformation advertises {current state version, minimum readable version}; host refuses (fail-closed, with remediation) to run an agent whose current version is below the stored state version; provider state is carried in the host envelope as an opaque, byte-preserved blob plus the host-owned indexed fields; UpgradeState failure restores the backup atomically and is receipted.
- **Acceptance test:** Conformance: write state at vN+1, load agent supporting only vN → typed refusal, no state modification; upgrade vN→vN+1 with injected failure → backup restored, receipt emitted; host round-trips state containing an unknown provider blob byte-identically.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 3, 4

#### ADV-054 — Lane 4 / pagination and observation completeness

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** MEDIUM  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** yes  **Affected phase/issues:** P1 message shape; P2/P4 acceptance
- **Claim challenged:** Proposal: observation reports whether a resource is 'present, missing, inaccessible, or ambiguous' and plans refuse ambiguous adoption; brief lane 4/scenario 37 requires pagination handling for uniqueness decisions.
- **Evidence:** provider-plugins.md never mentions pagination; canonical observation has no completeness/truncation marker; the broker requirements list response caps but no multi-page iteration contract; Stripe duplicate-endpoint recovery and 'existing same-URL endpoint is an unmanaged conflict' (Stripe acceptance) both require a provably complete listing to be sound.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Provider lists webhook endpoints, broker response cap or a single-page request truncates the listing, the matching endpoint is on page 2; Observe reports 'missing', Plan proposes CREATE, Apply creates a duplicate remote webhook — the exact drift/duplication the system exists to prevent, reported as success.
- **Impact:** Uniqueness- and adoption-safety guarantees are silently voided by truncation; affects Stripe and Resend webhook lifecycles.
- **Required change:** Add per-collection completeness to the canonical observation (complete | truncated(+reason)); require Plan to emit BLOCKED (or MANUAL_ACTION) when a uniqueness-dependent decision rests on a truncated listing; define broker pagination (either descriptor-level page iteration or host-side pagination with a declared cap) in the broker protocol.
- **Acceptance test:** Cassette with a two-page webhook listing where the match is on page 2: full iteration finds it (no CREATE planned); a forced-truncation replay yields BLOCKED, never CREATE.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 3, 4

#### ADV-055 — Lane 4 / Doctor duplicates Observe

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** MEDIUM  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1 protocol simplification; P2-P4 provider implementations
- **Claim challenged:** Proposal: Doctor is a separate RPC doing 'bounded read-only local/remote checks' including auth validity, remote resource health, and drift — all also produced by Observe ('Observation' section lists credential validity, resource presence, drift fields; 'Doctor integration' lists provider authentication and scope, remote resource health, drift).
- **Evidence:** Both sections of provider-plugins.md enumerate overlapping remote reads; nothing requires Doctor's remote half to share Observe's canonicalization or broker policy, creating two independently-policed remote read paths per provider.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Doctor's ad-hoc remote checks drift from Observe's canonical reads (different path templates, different rate-limit behavior); doctor reports healthy while plan sees drift, or doctor consumes rate limits that break a subsequent Observe; broker manifest must declare a second set of read paths that policy reviewers must audit separately.
- **Impact:** Duplicated vendor-read logic in every provider, doubled broker policy surface, and inconsistent health-vs-plan answers — an operability and audit cost with no capability gain.
- **Required change:** Simplify: Doctor's remote evidence must be host-composed from the same Observe call (host runs Observe, passes canonical observation into Doctor; Doctor's own network access limited to checks Observe cannot express, ideally none in v0). Keep Doctor for offline/local diagnostics and remediation mapping.
- **Acceptance test:** Conformance asserts Doctor with network denied still produces its full local section, and its remote-health section is byte-derived from a supplied Observe result; no broker request originates from Doctor in the default configuration.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 3, 4

#### ADV-056 — Lane 12 / endpoint resolution authority

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1, P2
- **Claim challenged:** 'Codefly resolves a loopback runtime origin' — implying one authoritative local resolution exists for provider callbacks.
- **Evidence:** Two resolutions exist and can disagree: the deterministic hash (core/network/port.go:117-132 ToNamedPort; core/network/runtime_manager.go:55-74 NativeFor) and the live-flow authoritative address; cli/cmd/common/endpoints.go:100-124 (ResolvePreferredNative) documents that 'container runtimes and persisted flow allocations... may legitimately bind a different host port than a fresh hash would predict' and SILENTLY falls back to the static hash when the flow daemon is unreachable. The hash also folds namingScope (runtime_manager.go:68-72), so resolving with the wrong scope yields a different port. The proposal never states which resolution provider operations must use or whether Apply requires a live flow.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Developer runs the starter under a containerized or scoped flow. Provider setup runs while the flow daemon is down (or without the scope): the host resolves the callback via the static hash, projects/forwards to port X, but the running service binds Y. The Stripe CLI forwards to X; every webhook delivery fails with connection refused; nothing in setup or plan flagged it because fallback is silent.
- **Impact:** Local-forwarded and local-direct modes — the core dogfood paths — can be wired to a port nothing listens on, with no error at setup time.
- **Required change:** Specify resolution authority in the protocol/host contract: for local-direct/local-forwarded operations the host must query the running flow and hard-fail (or explicitly warn with the exact `codefly run` remediation) when only static resolution is available; provider doctor must include a callback listening check (the primitive exists: Reachable, cli/cmd/common/endpoints.go:148-158, and `--require-up`, cli/cmd/endpoint/endpoint.go:89-91).
- **Acceptance test:** With no flow running, `codefly provider setup billing` in local-forwarded mode either refuses or emits a MANUAL_ACTION naming the unstarted service; with a containerized flow bound to a non-hash port, the projected/reported forward target matches the actually bound port.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 11, 12

#### ADV-057 — Lane 11 / atomic multi-file writes

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1
- **Claim challenged:** Writer invariant 6: 'fsync and atomically rename the complete output set'.
- **Evidence:** An output set spans at least two files (e.g. billing.env + billing.secret.env, proposal projection examples) inside the shared per-profile directory `configurations/<profile>/` (core/configurations/local_reader.go:64) that also contains other providers' and user-maintained files (module-saas-starter/configurations/local-dogfood/ holds 11+ files for six integrations). POSIX rename(2) is atomic per file only; swapping the whole directory would clobber unrelated files and race concurrent bindings. The behavioral seed is likewise non-atomic: setup_install_pair runs two separate `install -m 600` calls (provider-common.sh:146-174).
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Crash or SIGKILL between the public-file rename and the secret-file rename leaves billing.env at the new API version while billing.secret.env holds the old webhook secret; the loader has no pair-consistency notion (it merges whatever files exist, local_reader.go:474-509), so services start with a torn contract and webhook verification fails at runtime with no diagnostic pointing at the torn write.
- **Impact:** The one invariant that makes projection safe against crashes is stated as impossible-as-written, and no detection mechanism for a torn set is specified.
- **Required change:** Replace the invariant with an implementable transaction: stage in an owner-only temp dir, journal intended digests in provider state BEFORE renames, rename in defined order, verify digests after; on any start, doctor/loader-adjacent check compares on-disk digests to the journal and reports/repairs a torn set. State explicitly that cross-file atomicity is approximated, not guaranteed.
- **Acceptance test:** Tier-0 crash-injection test: kill the writer between the two renames; the next `codefly provider doctor` (and plan) must report the torn output set with the exact files and offer re-projection; a second apply converges with no manual file surgery.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 11, 12

#### ADV-058 — Lane 11 / ownership metadata and multi-provider contracts

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1
- **Claim challenged:** Writer attaches 'ownership metadata containing binding, provider, plan digest, contract version, and output hashes' and 'never overwrite[s] an unowned, differing configuration silently'.
- **Evidence:** The proposal never states where ownership metadata lives (workspace file? provider state under ~/.codefly/providers/? both?), whether it is Git-ignored or committed, or what happens when metadata is deleted while output files remain. It also never forbids two bindings in one environment declaring the same output-configuration name, and the loader silently appends values when multiple sources share a configuration name (core/configurations/local_reader.go:474-509 ConsolidateInfo merges env-style values without conflict for same-name infos). Compare-and-swap is specified against 'the output digest included in the approved plan' but the digest's storage location is likewise unspecified.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Two bindings (billing→stripe, billing-eu→stripe-eu) both declare output-configuration `billing`; or a user hand-edits billing.env after projection and ownership metadata was lost (fresh clone: state is under ~/.codefly, but if metadata is a workspace file it may be gitignored and absent). The writer either wrongly treats the file as unowned and blocks forever, or wrongly adopts it; a second provider's projection merges keys with the first at load time with no conflict surfaced.
- **Impact:** Ownership conflicts and provider-switching (stripe→paddle on the same `billing` contract) — explicitly in this lane's scope — have no defined resolution substrate; fresh-clone behavior is undefined.
- **Required change:** During foundation: pin ownership metadata to provider state (outside Git) keyed by workspace-sha/environment/binding with the output digest; host validation rejects two bindings in one environment sharing an output-configuration name; define the fresh-clone/lost-metadata flow (files present, no state → treated as unowned, require explicit adopt-projection); define provider switching as disconnect(old) then setup(new) with the writer refusing while the old ownership record is live.
- **Acceptance test:** Tier-0: environment YAML with two bindings naming output-configuration `billing` fails validation; delete provider state, rerun plan — output files are reported as unowned-existing and apply requires explicit adoption; disconnect then re-setup with a different provider succeeds and updates ownership.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 11, 12

#### ADV-059 — Lane 12 / forwarder companions (scenarios 32-adjacent)

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** MEDIUM  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P2, P4
- **Claim challenged:** 'Long-running forwarders are not provider resources... v1 reports the exact command and keeps lifecycle ownership separate' is a safe deferral for local dogfood and CI.
- **Evidence:** Resend CLI: 'Starts a server, registers a temporary webhook, streams events, and cleans up on exit' and CI auth is `--api-key` flag, RESEND_API_KEY env, or `resend login` keychain (https://resend.com/docs/cli) — the temporary webhook and its signing secret exist only per session. Stripe CLI: `stripe listen --forward-to` prints 'Your webhook signing secret is {{...}}' to the terminal (https://docs.stripe.com/webhooks); the proposal captures it 'through a safe host input' (manual paste, provider-plugins.md local-forwarded section). Goal 11 forbids secrets in command arguments; the founder-pain list includes 'paste credentials into shell command arguments'.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Every forwarder restart invalidates the projected webhook secret (certainly for Resend's temporary webhook; for Stripe the session secret's validity period is not documented on the fetched page — open vendor question). The developer restarts `resend webhooks listen`, the projected RESEND_WEBHOOK_SECRET is stale, signature verification silently rejects all events, and nothing in provider state, doctor, or receipts knows a forwarder exists. In CI without public ingress, someone supervises these CLIs by hand and the path of least resistance is `--api-key` on the command line — reintroducing the exact anti-pattern the system exists to remove, now outside all receipts and policy.
- **Impact:** The local dogfood and CI webhook paths depend on processes with no principal, no supervision, no receipts, and session-scoped secrets, and the proposal defers this without an acceptance story for staleness or CI auth.
- **Required change:** P2/P4 must specify: (a) how a stale forwarder-session secret is detected (doctor check comparing projected secret fingerprint against a re-captured session secret, or documented re-run of setup per forwarder session); (b) CI dogfood forwarder auth uses environment variables only, never flags; (c) an explicit statement that companions run outside Codefly authority in v1, surfaced in the plan as MANUAL_ACTION, so the deferral is visible rather than silent. Qualify Stripe listen-secret validity in live acceptance (LOW-confidence vendor gap).
- **Acceptance test:** Dogfood runbook test: restart the Resend forwarder, run `codefly provider doctor` — it must flag the stale webhook-secret projection with exact remediation; CI acceptance job runs both forwarders with env-var auth and `ps` output contains no credential material.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 11, 12

#### ADV-060 — Lane 11+12 / disconnect vs running consumers (scenario 33)

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1 (semantics text + receipt content), P5 (skew doctor check)
- **Claim challenged:** `codefly provider disconnect` 'removes or disables the output projection; retains provider state and all remote resources; reports what remains' — presented as a complete description of disconnect.
- **Evidence:** Configuration is injected into service processes as environment variables at start time (cli/pkg/orchestration/runner.go:273 → core/resources/environment_variables_manager.go:279-296, 489-513); no reload channel exists — a projection change is invisible to running processes until flow restart. The retained remote credential stays valid at the vendor (deletion-policy retain). The proposal's disconnect section (provider-plugins.md Deletion section) says nothing about running consumers or credential validity.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Operator disconnects the billing binding believing access is removed. The accounts service keeps STRIPE_API_KEY in its live environment and continues charging customers; the key remains valid at Stripe indefinitely. Days later an incident review finds 'disconnected' billing still mutating a vendor account, with receipts showing only a successful projection removal.
- **Impact:** Disconnect over-promises: it is a projection operation but reads as an access-removal operation; the gap is a real operational/security misunderstanding, not a corner case.
- **Required change:** Disconnect output must enumerate running services that currently hold the projected values (flow integration) and state explicitly that the remote credential remains valid; recommend/offer flow restart; document that true access removal is vendor-side key rotation or destroy. Doctor gains a projection/runtime-skew check (running-since timestamp vs projection digest change).
- **Acceptance test:** With the flow running, `codefly provider disconnect billing` output and receipt name each running consumer service still holding values and state that the Stripe key remains valid; doctor run before restart flags the skew; after restart the flag clears.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 11, 12

#### ADV-061 — deletion and ownership rules (contradiction)

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1 (binding schema, action semantics), P2 (Stripe destroy acceptance)
- **Claim challenged:** Remote deletion semantics are consistent across the action vocabulary, the binding schema, and the destroy command.
- **Evidence:** They conflict. core/docs/provider-plugins.md:398: '`provider.delete` | critical | Delete exactly one owned remote resource' — owned only. But destroy (1310-1313): 'creates a destructive plan naming exact owned/adopted resources; ... deletes only resources allowed by `delete-owned`' — the plan names adopted resources too, while the gating policy value is literally named delete-OWNED. Binding table (802): 'deletion-policy | `retain` by default; `delete-owned` only with explicit destroy' — ambiguous whether destroy requires the YAML value to be edited to delete-owned first, or the destroy command itself supplies/overrides it. Stripe acceptance (~1663) 'explicit destroy deletes only the owned test endpoint' presumes deletion succeeds without ever mentioning the deletion-policy prerequisite.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Case A: operator imports (adopts) a pre-existing production webhook, later runs destroy; one reading (destroy plan 'naming exact owned/adopted resources') deletes a resource the team created outside Codefly, the other reading (provider.delete = 'exactly one owned') refuses. Case B: binding keeps default deletion-policy retain; operator runs `codefly provider destroy`; per 1313 nothing is 'allowed by delete-owned', so destroy either deletes nothing (surprising, contradicts the acceptance criterion) or the command implicitly flips the policy (contradicting 'retain by default' and brief invariant 23).
- **Impact:** Deletion is the highest-risk operation class; two implementers or two reviewers reading this text will disagree on whether adopted resources are deletable and on what destroy does under the default policy — an operability and safety ambiguity, though fixable without protocol redesign.
- **Required change:** State normatively: (1) whether adopted resources are deletable via destroy (recommended: yes, but only with a distinct approval line item flagging non-Codefly origin, or no, with guidance to disconnect instead); (2) that destroy prompts/requires deletion-policy consent at plan time rather than requiring a prior YAML edit, or the opposite; (3) align the provider.delete action description ('owned') with whichever answer is chosen.
- **Acceptance test:** A conformance fixture with one owned and one adopted resource under deletion-policy retain runs destroy: the documented behavior (refuse / prompt / delete-with-flag) matches for both resources, and provider.delete admission logs show the ownership class evaluated.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lane 24

#### ADV-062 — output classification (secret described as both public and sensitive)

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1 (classification schema and writer tests), P3 (Sentry projection)
- **Claim challenged:** Each projected output has one classification driving Git-ignore checks, file placement, redaction, and cassette sanitization.
- **Evidence:** The Sentry projection example classifies the same DSN value both ways simultaneously: core/docs/provider-plugins.md:948-953 puts 'NEXT_PUBLIC_SENTRY_DSN=<public DSN>' in the public error-tracking.env, while 955-961 puts 'SENTRY_DSN=<public DSN used by backend SDK>' in 'error-tracking.secret.env or error-tracking.secret.ref.env' — the secret sink file — with an explicit comment that it is the same public DSN. Additionally 1522-1525 says 'Browser-exposable values such as a public Sentry DSN are redacted in generic output unless an explicit `--show-sensitive` policy permits display', treating the value operationally as sensitive while the vendor table (224) and Sentry design (1707-1709) call it public/browser-exposable. The proposal itself demands single classification: outputs carry 'classification' and 'whether browser exposure is allowed' as separate fields (914-924), and Tier-1 cassettes 'fail if a secret-shaped field remains' (1855).
- **Failure or attack sequence:** If DSN is SECRET, the writer's 'verify secret targets are Git-ignored' invariant (978) and the cassette sanitizer treat it as leak-on-sight — yet the identical bytes sit in the sibling public env file and in every browser bundle, so sanitizer/doctor checks either false-positive on the public copy or the 'fail if a secret-shaped field remains' rule is unenforceable for DSNs. If DSN is PUBLIC, the secret-file placement at 957 violates the sink's purpose and normalizes putting public values through the high-risk provider.project.secret action (402), inflating approval noise.
- **Impact:** The first projection-only provider (Sentry, P3) ships with an example that breaks the classification invariants the foundation tests must enforce; downstream, doctor, sanitizer, and redaction behavior become inconsistent.
- **Required change:** Classify DSN once — PUBLIC with browser-exposable=true — and move SENTRY_DSN into the public error-tracking.env in the example; reserve *.secret.env for SENTRY_AUTH_TOKEN only. Rewrite 1522-1525 to distinguish display policy (SENSITIVE-style redaction of public identifiers in terminal output) from storage classification, since the proposal's own input taxonomy (838-845) already separates PUBLIC/SENSITIVE/SECRET.
- **Acceptance test:** The Sentry conformance projection writes the DSN only to the public file; the cassette sanitizer and doctor Git-ignore checks pass on a workspace containing that projection without DSN-related suppressions.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lane 24

#### ADV-063 — host/provider responsibility assigned to both (idempotency keys)

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** yes  **Affected phase/issues:** P1 (broker and request-descriptor schema), P2 (Stripe retries)
- **Claim challenged:** Idempotency responsibility is cleanly assigned.
- **Evidence:** It is assigned to both sides. Provider owns 'idempotency strategy' in the reuse boundary (core/docs/provider-plugins.md:291). The broker requirements assign the host 'request idempotency-key injection where declared' (1440), and the brief's summary says the broker 'injects stable idempotency keys where declared' (brief:296). But the Apply contract commands the provider itself to 'use the operation/resource identity as the vendor idempotency key where supported' (1179) — an act the provider cannot perform if the broker strips caller headers and injects the key, and which the broker cannot verify if the provider composes request bodies/headers.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Implementation A (provider sets the key in its request descriptor): a buggy or malicious provider varies the key across retries of the same operation, turning an ambiguous Stripe outcome into a duplicate webhook endpoint — defeating the safety property the design attributes to stable keys; the broker has no rule authorizing it to reject the mismatch. Implementation B (broker derives and injects): the provider MUST at 1179 is unsatisfiable and conformance tests written against it fail. The two teams building P1 (host) and P2 (Stripe provider) can each implement their half correctly per the text and produce a system where either both or neither set the header.
- **Impact:** Retry-safety of mutations — a core reliability claim — depends on which of the two contradictory owners an implementer picks; also a confused-deputy surface if the provider may set the header at all.
- **Required change:** Assign key derivation and injection exclusively to the broker: the manifest/request-descriptor declares which resource-type+method pairs support idempotency; the broker derives the key from (operation ID, resource address) and injects it; provider-supplied Idempotency-Key (and equivalent) headers join Authorization/cookie/proxy on the forbidden list (1438-1439). Rewrite 291 as 'idempotency capability declaration' and 1179 as 'declare idempotency support per action so the host injects a stable key'.
- **Acceptance test:** A Tier-2 conformance case retries an interrupted create: the broker-injected key is byte-identical across attempts; a provider request descriptor carrying its own Idempotency-Key header is rejected by the broker with a policy failure, not forwarded.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lane 24

#### ADV-064 — acceptance criteria requiring infrastructure the proposal declines to own (completeness gap)

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** MEDIUM  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P2 (Stripe CI acceptance), P4 (Resend), Tier-4 dogfood
- **Claim challenged:** P2's exit gate and CI acceptance are executable as specified: 'a founder can ... exercise checkout and webhook lifecycle' (core/docs/provider-plugins.md:2082-2086), P2 delivers a 'CI acceptance entry point' (2078), and Tier 4 dogfood includes 'Stripe sandbox checkout, portal, webhook, reconciliation' and 'Resend invitation and delivery lifecycle' (1900-1903).
- **Evidence:** The architecture explicitly refuses to own the only two mechanisms that can deliver a remote webhook to a local/CI process: non-goals exclude 'own tunnels, DNS, or long-running local webhook forwarders' and 'install or invoke vendor CLIs from inside a provider agent' (128-130), and the endpoint section confirms 'Long-running forwarders are not provider resources... v1 reports the exact command and keeps lifecycle ownership separate' (1370-1373); local-forwarded mode says 'Stripe CLI is run separately with --forward-to' (1625-1627) with no named runner. `public` mode requires environment ingress, and nothing in the document provides or discusses public HTTPS ingress for CI. The brief's Lane 12 explicitly asks about 'CI dogfood without public ingress'; the proposal never answers. Confidence MEDIUM only because a human-in-the-loop dogfood works fine (the operator runs the printed command) — the gap is specifically unattended CI.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** P2 lands the CI acceptance entry point; the pipeline reaches the webhook lifecycle step; no process is responsible for running `stripe listen --forward-to` (forbidden inside the provider, unowned by the host) and no public ingress exists in CI, so webhook delivery never occurs; the team either quietly adds an unspecified, unsandboxed vendor-CLI supervisor (new trusted component with credential handling — the Stripe CLI listener secret — outside the reviewed threat model) or the exit gate is waived.
- **Impact:** The P2 and Tier-4 acceptance criteria depend on infrastructure that is postponed/refused by the same document, so either the gates are unenforceable in CI or scope silently expands into an unreviewed forwarder supervisor.
- **Required change:** P2 must name the CI webhook mechanism explicitly: either (a) a test-harness-owned forwarder supervisor declared as test infrastructure (with its secret-capture path specified, since `stripe listen` emits a signing secret), (b) CI runs public-mode against a real ingress with the environment that provides it named, or (c) the CI entry point's webhook assertions are downgraded to replay/cassette tier and the live webhook lifecycle is human-run — stated in the exit gate.
- **Acceptance test:** The P2 CI acceptance job runs unattended from a clean environment and either completes a real webhook round-trip via the named mechanism or the exit gate text no longer claims it does.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lane 24

#### ADV-065 — Approval / escalation for human CLI

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P0, P1, P5
- **Claim challenged:** Manifest risk levels feed the existing escalation system and 'an approval binds the principal, grantor, … plan digest, exact actions/resources' for provider.create (high) and provider.delete (critical) (proposal:544-560, 396-403).
- **Evidence:** No core PDP emits PDPDecision.RequireApproval (rg 'RequireApproval: true' over non-test code = none; observability.go:224 'Until then, RequireApproval stays false … no PDP returns this'). RequireApproval is produced only by the backend's PermissionsBackend.Decide (saas-starter) and consumed by the agent-SDK escalation flow (escalation.go:40-52, MIND_INTEGRATION.md); EscalationGrantor is implemented only in module-saas-starter host.go, not in the CLI. PermissionPolicy.RiskLevelOf exists (permissions.go:472) but nothing maps a risk level to a RequireApproval decision.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Scenario 10 / human-CLI: a `codefly provider destroy` (critical) runs with no interactive grantor approval because the only escalation path is the Mind agent SDK + a saas-starter grantor; a human at a CLI has no escalation channel wired, so either the action is silently allowed by the role grant or it is hard-denied with no approval route.
- **Impact:** The proposal's approval-binds-plan-digest guarantee for high/critical provider actions is unimplemented for the primary (human CLI) authority context; risk_levels are advisory only.
- **Required change:** Either (a) implement a CLI-side approval path that turns high/critical plan actions into an interactive confirmation bound to the plan digest AND a policy RequireApproval where the backend supports it, or (b) explicitly scope v1 to 'role-grant only, no synchronous approval' and remove the approval-binding invariant from v1. Do not claim escalation reuse without a CLI grantor.
- **Acceptance test:** Test that a critical action with no approving grantor is denied (not allowed) and surfaces an actionable escalation instruction; and that when approval is granted, the minted authorization's plan/approval caveats match the exact plan digest.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lane 5, Lane 2 (policy)

#### ADV-066 — Resource vocabulary / prefix expressiveness / ambient CRUD

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1
- **Claim challenged:** 'Resources use a hierarchical form so the existing exact/prefix permission matching remains useful … The provider manifest can declare a provider-wide prefix; role grants and operator policies narrow it' (proposal:411-428); manifest example declares provider.create/update/delete on resource `provider:stripe/*` (proposal:712-724).
- **Evidence:** PermissionPolicy matching uses globMatch, which supports only bare `*`, a single trailing `prefix*`, or exact strings; validateGlobPattern explicitly rejects mid-string or multiple `*` (permissions.go:415-428, 489-498). The chosen resource form `provider:<provider>/<workspace>/<environment>/<binding>/<type>/<remote-id>` with the manifest's `provider:stripe/*` prefix-matches EVERY workspace, environment, binding, type, and remote-id.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** A single role grant of provider.delete on `provider:stripe/*` (mirroring the manifest ceiling shape the doc models) authorizes deletion of any Stripe resource in any workspace/environment/binding. You cannot ceiling 'any binding within one environment' because that needs a mid-path wildcard, which the matcher rejects — operators are pushed toward the broad trailing form.
- **Impact:** One broad grant yields ambient remote CRUD across all of a provider's resources; the narrowing the proposal relies on is only expressible as fully-qualified exact prefixes, not partial-path patterns.
- **Required change:** Require that production ceilings and grants bind at least workspace+environment+binding as an exact prefix (remote-id last makes `provider:stripe/<ws>/<env>/<binding>/<type>/*` expressible — mandate it). Add a conformance/admission check that rejects a production provider.* grant whose resource is `provider:<p>/*` or bare `*`. Document that mid-path scoping is unsupported by the matcher.
- **Acceptance test:** Permission-matrix test: a grant of provider.delete on `provider:stripe/*` is rejected by production admission; a grant scoped to `provider:stripe/<ws>/<env>/<binding>/stripe.webhook-endpoint/*` is accepted and does not match a different binding's resource.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lane 5, Lane 2 (policy)

#### ADV-067 — Bypasses: break-glass, AllowAll, PDP-off must be rejected by provider production admission

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1, P5
- **Claim challenged:** 'No production provider invocation may use WithoutPrincipal, WithoutSandbox, AllowAllPDP, shadow-only enforcement, or break-glass implicitly. Those paths … must be greppable, surfaced in diagnostics, and rejected by provider production admission' (proposal:382-385; invariant 10).
- **Evidence:** WithProductionAdmission validates only sandbox(non-native)+principal(valid,non-expired)+permissionsCallback+scopedAuthSecret>=32B (loader.go:609-632). It does NOT inspect CODEFLY_BREAK_GLASS_JUSTIFICATION (hardening.go:170-238, process-wide bypass logged only at WARN) nor CODEFLY_PDP_MODE=off/shadow (pdp_mode.go:65-88, 113-130 where off→AllowAllPDP and shadow→always-allow). A host that sets PDP mode off/shadow or break-glass would pass production admission yet perform no real enforcement.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Scenario-adjacent to 28: an operator runs the provider CLI with CODEFLY_PDP_MODE=shadow (or break-glass set for an unrelated incident); every provider mutation is allowed while appearing 'admitted', because admission never checks these process-wide bypasses.
- **Impact:** Production mutations can proceed with enforcement silently disabled; invariant 10 is unmet by the existing admission gate.
- **Required change:** Extend provider production admission to also assert PDP mode == enforce and break-glass inactive (or require an explicit, receipted break-glass operation), and to emit these facts into the receipt and doctor output.
- **Acceptance test:** Admission test: production provider Apply refuses to start when CODEFLY_PDP_MODE != enforce or CODEFLY_BREAK_GLASS_JUSTIFICATION is set, unless an explicit governed break-glass flag with a receipt is present.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lane 5, Lane 2 (policy)

#### ADV-068 — Lane 1 — value case / founder UX

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P0 (framing), P2 (acceptance criteria)
- **Claim challenged:** Founder UX materially improves in v1 local dogfood ('founders should not have to manually copy dynamic local ports; paste credentials...').
- **Evidence:** Current scripts already resolve ports via codefly endpoint (module-saas-starter/scripts/setup/provider-common.sh:104-114; workos.sh:187-191), take hidden/file/env credential input never via argv (stripe.sh:129-134), assert Git-ignore (provider-common.sh:135-144), install 0600 atomically from a temp dir (provider-common.sh:146-174), and run doctor (provider-common.sh:176-183). Meanwhile v1 keeps the biggest local frictions manual: vendor CLI forwarders are 'separately supervised companions' the founder runs by hand (core/docs/provider-plugins.md:1370-1373), the Stripe local-forwarded signing secret is still 'captured through a safe host input' i.e. pasted (:1624-1629), and dashboard key creation, Resend DNS verification, and WorkOS redirect registration remain manual.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** A founder running local dogfood in P2 experiences nearly the same number of manual steps as with stripe.sh today (create key in dashboard, run stripe listen, paste whsec, approve plan); if the design is sold on founder UX, the delta observed at P2 exit will be plan display + rerun-no-diff, inviting the conclusion that the system failed, when its actual value (credential-purpose separation, state/ownership, drift, production path) is real but different.
- **Impact:** Mis-anchored value case distorts prioritization: it argues for spending on setup ergonomics instead of on the demonstrable current harms — management/setup credentials projected into runtime today (sentry.sh:203, stripe.sh:219, workos.sh:347-349) and zero remote-resource state (stripe.sh:196 discards the created endpoint ID).
- **Required change:** Re-state the primary v1 value as credential hygiene + lifecycle state + production path; add the concrete current-script credential leaks as motivating evidence; scope a P2 UX acceptance metric honestly (steps removed vs stripe.sh, not 'one command').
- **Acceptance test:** P2 exit gate lists the exact manual steps remaining in local-forwarded Stripe setup and shows runtime projection contains a restricted key, not the management key — a property the current script fails.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 1, 23

#### ADV-069 — Lane 1/4 — protocol frozen against trivially simple resources

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** yes  **Affected phase/issues:** P1, P2 (freeze point), P3-P4
- **Claim challenged:** Designing against Stripe, Sentry, and Resend before freezing v0 sufficiently pressures the lifecycle abstraction.
- **Evidence:** The entire v1 managed-resource inventory is two single-object webhook endpoints: cross-provider matrix 'managed create/update: webhook | not required | webhook' (core/docs/provider-plugins.md:1918); Sentry v0.1 is observe/project only (:1697-1701) and exercises none of CREATE/UPDATE/REPLACE/DELETE/import-conflict machinery; both webhook resources are singleton, non-paginated, dependency-free, synchronous objects.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** IMPORT, REPLACE, delete-before-create ordering, ownership conflict, drift comparison, and UpgradeState semantics are frozen in v0 with at most one nontrivial exercise (Stripe webhook update); the first provider with a list-valued or dependent resource (WorkOS redirect-URI set, Sentry project + client-key pair, feature flags) forces breaking protocol changes exactly where the proposal claims stability ('the protocol is designed against all three initial providers before being declared stable' — but two of three barely use it).
- **Impact:** High probability of a v1 protocol break shortly after GA, contradicting invariant 35's intent.
- **Required change:** Either (a) add one deliberately harder managed resource to the pre-freeze design cohort (Sentry project creation, or WorkOS redirect URIs as a paper design exercise), or (b) explicitly mark REPLACE/IMPORT/UpgradeState message shapes as capability-negotiated/unstable in v0 and freeze only Validate/Observe/Plan/Apply(CREATE,UPDATE,DELETE)/Project.
- **Acceptance test:** Before v0 is declared frozen, a documented paper-fit of one list-valued or dependent resource exists and required no message-shape change, or the unstable-capability markers are present in provider.proto.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 1, 23

#### ADV-070 — Lane 1/20 — too narrow to replace scripts

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P2-P5
- **Claim challenged:** The provider system replaces the setup-script mechanism ('scripts become compatibility shims').
- **Evidence:** The starter has 8 integration scripts totaling 1813 lines (workos.sh 377, posthog.sh 213, turnstile.sh 207, otel.sh 139, plus stripe/sentry/resend/common); the funded roadmap P2-P4 migrates only 3 (core/docs/provider-plugins.md:2077, 2102, 2133); WorkOS, PostHog, Turnstile, OTLP are explicitly 'later validation cohorts, not reasons to expand v1' (:2177-2189). Shim conversion is gated on per-provider parity (brief invariant 32).
- **Failure or attack sequence:** After P4, founders operate a split system indefinitely: `codefly provider setup billing` for Stripe but `scripts/setup/workos.sh` for identity — two credential-ingestion styles, two doctor stories, two failure surfaces — and the shared shell library (provider-common.sh) must be maintained in parallel with the Go coordinator, doubling the security-sensitive surface the proposal aims to eliminate.
- **Impact:** The core duplication argument ('waiting longer would copy the same security-sensitive shell lifecycle into every starter', provider-plugins.md:78-79) is only half-resolved by the funded scope; the mixed state is the long-run steady state unless the tail is explicitly cheap to onboard.
- **Required change:** Add a roadmap statement for the script tail: either budget a P4.5 wave for the four remaining integrations (note that Turnstile/OTLP/PostHog are validate+project only and should each cost days, which is itself evidence for a lighter provider tier), or declare the mixed state acceptable with an owner and sunset criteria.
- **Acceptance test:** Roadmap names the disposition (provider, shim, or retained script) and target phase for all 8 existing integrations, not just 3.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 1, 23

#### ADV-071 — Lane 23 — credential auth-scheme extensibility

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** MEDIUM  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** yes  **Affected phase/issues:** P1 (message shapes), post-GA providers
- **Claim challenged:** Credential handles bound to 'origin and authorization/header placement' plus broker header injection cover provider APIs, while 'provider-specific signature algorithms and response parsing remain in the provider agent'.
- **Evidence:** core/docs/provider-plugins.md:475-477 (handle binds header placement), :1443-1444 (signature algorithms remain in the provider agent). These two statements are jointly unsatisfiable for request-signing APIs: a signature over the canonical request (SigV4-style object storage, some managed-database control planes) requires the raw secret at signing time, which the handle model forbids the provider from having. Inference labeled MEDIUM: the SigV4 requirement is well-known but was not re-verified against current AWS/Cloudflare primary docs in this review.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** First object-storage or AWS-family provider: either the broker must implement vendor signing (contradicting :1443 and requiring a broker release per new scheme) or the provider receives raw credential bytes (destroying the handle boundary). If the v0 request-descriptor/handle messages hard-code 'header placement', supporting signing later is a breaking protocol change.
- **Impact:** Immediate break for 2 of the 12 listed future providers (object storage, managed Postgres on cloud-signed APIs); OAuth-consent providers break similarly because the secret-source list (:869-877) has no interactive acquisition or refresh path.
- **Required change:** In v0, make the credential-handle auth mechanism an extensible enum (BEARER_HEADER, BASIC, QUERY, SIGNED{scheme} reserved) with broker-side signing declared as a capability; document OAuth acquisition/refresh as an explicit non-goal with a named extension point so ProviderContext does not preclude it. Do not implement signing in v1.
- **Acceptance test:** provider.proto credential-handle and request-descriptor messages compile with a new auth-scheme value added without breaking existing providers; a conformance test rejects a provider requesting an undeclared scheme.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 1, 23

#### ADV-072 — Lane 18 — doctor extension breaks the existing workspace-doctor contract and is unbounded against shared vendor rate limits

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1 (CLI doctor integration)
- **Claim challenged:** The reuse table row 'workspace doctor … required extension: provider installation, auth, remote health, drift sections' and the 'Doctor integration' section say `codefly doctor workspace --env X` should include 12 provider sections including remote resource health and drift (core/docs/provider-plugins.md).
- **Evidence:** cli/cmd/doctor_workspace.go:92-95: the readiness check 'must not create, modify, or delete any file, and must not start agents, containers, or services'; :684-686 repeats this in user-facing help; :24-27 makes code changes a breaking JSON contract change requiring a doctorWorkspaceSchemaVersion bump (:46); :757 default overall timeout is 30s. Provider sections require starting provider agents and making per-binding vendor calls. Resend's official docs: 'The default maximum rate limit is 10 requests per second per team… across all API keys', 429 on excess (https://resend.com/docs/api-reference/introduction). No per-check deadline, per-binding request cap, offline degradation, or caching is specified anywhere in the proposal's doctor text; 'bounded read-only checks' is asserted without a mechanism.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** A CI pipeline that already runs `codefly doctor workspace --json` per job (its designed use per cli/docs/doctor-workspace-qualification.md) silently starts spawning provider agents and making vendor calls: runs blow the 30s budget, consume the team-wide Resend request budget and throttle a concurrent real `provider apply` with 429s, and worktree automation matching on the versioned JSON schema breaks because sections/codes changed without a schema bump. Offline laptops see doctor fail entirely instead of degrading to local checks.
- **Impact:** Regression of an existing, qualified, documented CLI contract; doctor becomes slow, rate-limit-hostile, and unusable offline — the exact 'unbounded collection of expensive provider calls' the brief tells this lane to prevent.
- **Required change:** Keep `codefly doctor workspace` default behavior local-static (binding schema, agent availability, input config presence, endpoint resolution, projection digest — no agent spawn, no network). Remote provider checks live in `codefly provider doctor` and are included in workspace doctor only behind an explicit `--providers` flag with: schemaVersion bump to 2, per-binding max-request count declared in the provider manifest, per-check deadline composed under the global --timeout, Retry-After-aware skip with a distinct 'skipped: rate-limited' status, and a distinct 'remote checks skipped (offline/unauthorized)' status separate from not_ready.
- **Acceptance test:** With one binding and no network, `codefly doctor workspace --json` completes under the existing 30s bound with schema_version bumped, no agent process spawned (assert via process table in test), and remote sections marked skipped rather than failing the report; with --providers and a rate-limited fixture, remote checks stop after the manifest-declared request cap.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 16, 17, 18

#### ADV-073 — Lane 18 / scenario 40 — diagnostic code taxonomy: no policy-denied code, and 'provider' namespace collision

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1
- **Claim challenged:** The proposal's stable diagnostic code list (provider.config.missing, provider.auth.rejected, provider.auth.scope-insufficient, …, core/docs/provider-plugins.md 'Doctor integration') is sufficient for stable automation and operator remediation.
- **Evidence:** The proposed list contains vendor-side codes only: `provider.auth.rejected` / `provider.auth.scope-insufficient` describe the VENDOR rejecting a credential; there is no code for the Codefly PDP denying the principal (`provider.observe` role missing) or for approval-required. Meanwhile the existing doctor's stable codes `provider_not_configured`, `provider_executable_missing`, `provider_authentication_required`, `provider_resolution_failed` (cli/cmd/doctor_workspace.go:37-40) mean SECRET provider (1Password) — the proposal's own terminology table warns 'secret provider … is not an external provider agent', yet both code families would appear in one doctor report with 'provider' meaning two different things.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Scenario 40: a principal with configuration-inspection rights but no `provider.observe` grant runs doctor. The only expressible outcome today is a vendor-shaped auth code or a generic failure, so the operator 'fixes' a perfectly valid Stripe key instead of requesting a role grant; automation keyed on `provider_authentication_required` (secret provider) also matches or mis-matches the new external-provider failures.
- **Impact:** Wrong remediation paths for the most common support case (permissions), and a breaking ambiguity in the machine-readable doctor contract that is expensive to rename after automation depends on it.
- **Required change:** Before P1 ships the JSON contract: add `provider.policy.denied` and `provider.approval.required` codes distinct from vendor auth codes; namespace the external-provider code family unambiguously (e.g. `extprovider.*` or `provider-binding.*`) or rename the existing secret-provider doctor codes to `secret_provider_*` under a schema bump. Define scenario-40 semantics: local sections run, remote sections report `provider.policy.denied` with the missing action named, report status distinguishes 'partially checked' from 'not ready'.
- **Acceptance test:** Doctor run under a principal lacking provider.observe yields a report where local checks are ok, each remote section carries provider.policy.denied naming the missing action, no credential handle was minted (assert via broker audit), and exit/status codes distinguish this from a vendor 401 fixture.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 16, 17, 18

#### ADV-074 — Lane 16 — Observe completeness under pagination and broker body caps

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** MEDIUM  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** yes  **Affected phase/issues:** P1 (protocol), P2/P4 acceptance
- **Claim challenged:** Observation returns 'whether a resource is present, missing, inaccessible, or ambiguous' and this is sufficient for safe planning (core/docs/provider-plugins.md 'Observation'; Stripe acceptance 'existing same-URL endpoint is an unmanaged conflict').
- **Evidence:** The observation result enumeration has no 'enumeration incomplete/truncated' state. The broker mandates 'smaller default body caps for provider APIs' (baseline today is 4 MiB, toolbox-web/server.go:21,251-257) and rate-limit denial mid-operation is expected, so a multi-page webhook/domain listing can be cut off between pages. The unmanaged-conflict and duplicate-endpoint-recovery acceptance criteria both require a COMPLETE enumeration to be sound; nothing requires the provider to report completeness or the host to check it.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Resend webhook list needs 3 pages; page 2 request hits the team-wide 429 or the body cap; provider reports the desired webhook 'missing'; Plan emits CREATE; approval and Apply follow; a duplicate webhook now exists, and the pre-existing one becomes a latent unmanaged conflict — violating invariant 31 (second run no-diff) without any component technically misbehaving.
- **Impact:** Silent duplicate remote resources and phantom drift under ordinary rate-limiting — precisely the 'partial pagination' failure this lane exists to catch.
- **Required change:** Add a per-resource-type `enumeration: complete | truncated` marker to the Observe result (protocol message, P1); host rule: a plan computed from a truncated enumeration may not contain CREATE or DELETE for that resource type — it must emit BLOCKED with a retry/remediation diagnostic; broker must surface truncation (cap hit, pagination aborted) distinctly from success.
- **Acceptance test:** Cassette with a paginated list truncated at page 2 → Observe returns truncated, Plan returns BLOCKED for that resource type, no CREATE is offered; completing the cassette yields the normal plan.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 16, 17, 18

#### ADV-075 — Lane 17 — plan-file portability and CI plan→apply contract unspecified

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1 (CLI + plan format)
- **Claim challenged:** `codefly provider plan BINDING --env ENV [--out PLAN]` followed by `codefly provider apply --plan PLAN` is a workable CI/automation split (core/docs/provider-plugins.md 'Commands').
- **Evidence:** The plan digest binds prior-state digest, observed-state digest, resolved endpoint identities, output digest, and secret-sink identity ('Plan' section), and provider state lives under `~/.codefly/providers/<canonical-workspace-path-sha256>/<environment>/<binding>/` ('Location and durability'). Nothing states that plan and apply must share the same state directory, host, or workspace canonical path; nothing defines a plan TTL, the plan file's trust status at apply time, or a secret-shape scan on plan files whose 'before/after safe canonical fields' are provider-authored and host-unverifiable.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** A CI pipeline plans in job A (runner 1, workspace at /home/ci/build-17) and applies in job B (runner 2, /home/ci/build-18, empty ~/.codefly/providers): the workspace-path sha and prior-state digest can never match, so apply always rejects — or, if the plan file is made self-contained to 'fix' this, apply bypasses local state and locking entirely. Separately, a buggy provider embeds an observed sensitive field in a plan action's 'before' fields and the plan file is committed to CI artifacts.
- **Impact:** The advertised CI workflow either cannot work or works by silently weakening the state/locking model; plan artifacts become an unscanned secret-exfiltration surface.
- **Required change:** State normatively in P1: plan and apply must execute against the same provider state store and canonical workspace path; the plan file is a display/transport artifact — at apply the host re-derives the digest from local state and the file's embedded digest must match (file is never independently trusted); define plan TTL = scoped-authorization TTL; run the cassette secret-shape scanner over plan files before writing `--out`; document the supported CI pattern (same-runner plan+apply, or re-plan with --expect-digest gating).
- **Acceptance test:** Apply of a plan file against a state directory whose prior-state digest differs fails with provider.plan.stale; a plan file containing a seeded secret-shaped string fails the write with the sanitizer error; documented same-runner CI flow passes end to end.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 16, 17, 18

#### ADV-076 — Lane 17 — exit codes and terminal machine outcomes unspecified

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** MEDIUM  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1
- **Claim challenged:** 'Every read/plan/doctor command supports deterministic JSON' and machine output lists terminal state and next actions, which the proposal treats as sufficient for CI (core/docs/provider-plugins.md 'Machine output').
- **Evidence:** No exit-code table exists anywhere in the proposal. The existing doctor already commits to a documented exit contract (0 ready / 1 failed, cli/cmd/doctor_workspace.go:690-692) precisely because automation must not parse prose. P5 promises a 'drift-only CI command', which is unusable without a distinct diff-present exit code (Terraform's -detailed-exitcode precedent — inference from ecosystem practice, not verified here).
- **Failure or attack sequence:** A CI drift gate shells out to `codefly provider plan`; exit 0 with a non-empty diff and exit 0 with no diff are indistinguishable without fragile JSON parsing conventions that each pipeline invents; UNCERTAIN and policy-denied apply outcomes both surface as generic exit 1, so retry automation treats an UNCERTAIN mutation like a transient error and re-runs apply.
- **Impact:** Every downstream automation invents its own outcome detection; the dangerous case (UNCERTAIN misread as retryable failure) directly enables the duplicate-mutation path of finding 1.
- **Required change:** Define in the P1 CLI contract: exit 0 = success/no-diff; 2 = plan diff present; 3 = partial; 4 = uncertain; 5 = denied/approval-required; 6 = stale plan/digest mismatch; 1 = command error — mirrored by a closed `status` enum in the JSON payload, with the same breaking-change discipline as doctorWorkspaceSchemaVersion.
- **Acceptance test:** Golden tests assert each terminal state maps to its documented exit code and JSON status; an UNCERTAIN apply fixture exits 4 and a wrapper retry script demonstrably does not re-run apply.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 16, 17, 18

#### ADV-077 — Lane 17 — approval UX undefined for solo-founder and CI contexts

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** MEDIUM  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P0 decision, P1 implementation
- **Claim challenged:** 'PDPDecision.RequireApproval and escalation: pause high-risk or critical provider actions for an explicit grantor decision' plus '--yes … cannot manufacture authority' fully specify approval (core/docs/provider-plugins.md 'Approval and revocation semantics').
- **Evidence:** The proposal binds an approval to principal, grantor, plan digest, expiry, and approval ID, but never says who the grantor is in an interactive local session (the same human? a second party?), whether self-approval satisfies escalation for a solo founder, or how a CI service principal obtains an approval for a high-risk action non-interactively (pre-issued approval artifact? out-of-band console?). The founder flow ('setup … apply only after confirmation') implies same-terminal confirmation, which either IS the approval (making escalation decorative locally) or is not (making high-risk local dogfood actions — every webhook create is declared high risk — impossible for a single founder).
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Founder runs `codefly provider setup billing --env local-dogfood`; the webhook CREATE is risk=high, policy requires approval; there is no second grantor; setup blocks forever or the implementation quietly collapses approval into the interactive prompt, meaning production later inherits an approval path that was never really exercised.
- **Impact:** The primary advertised UX (one setup command) is unimplementable as specified, or the approval mechanism is vacuous locally and untested before production needs it.
- **Required change:** Decide in P0/P1: local/interactive context = same-principal explicit confirmation recorded as an approval receipt with approval ID (self-approval allowed where operator policy permits); production/CI = out-of-band approval bound to the plan digest with TTL, presented to `apply` as an artifact/reference; document both flows and test the CI flow in the P1 permission matrix.
- **Acceptance test:** Local dogfood: setup completes with a recorded self-approval receipt naming grantor=principal; CI: apply with a valid plan-digest-bound approval artifact succeeds, with an expired or wrong-digest artifact exits denied without remote change.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 16, 17, 18

#### ADV-078 — Lane 18 — secret-shape scanning specified only for cassettes, not diagnostics/receipts/support bundles

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** MEDIUM  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1 (host string ingestion), P5 (bundle)
- **Claim challenged:** 'Receipts never include credentials, request/response bodies, webhook secrets, DSNs, email addresses, or arbitrary vendor errors' and diagnostics are 'bounded' (core/docs/provider-plugins.md 'Apply', 'Required controls' #19).
- **Evidence:** These are assertions about provider behavior with no host-side enforcement: FailureDiagnostic.message (core/proto/codefly/base/v0/failure.proto:126-127) and provider remediation text are provider-authored free strings that flow into doctor output, receipt context, and the P5 support bundle ('safe manifests, digests, receipts, and diagnostics'). The 'fail if a secret-shaped field remains' scanner is specified only for cassettes (Tier 1). A buggy — not even malicious — provider that embeds a vendor error body containing a key fragment into a diagnostic message defeats every downstream 'safe' surface at once.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Provider maps a Stripe 401 by interpolating the raw vendor error JSON (which echoes the key prefix and request body) into FailureDiagnostic.message; the host persists it into the receipt and doctor report; the P5 support bundle ships it to a support channel.
- **Impact:** Single point of failure for the 'no secrets in receipts/diagnostics/support bundles' guarantee across brief invariants 18 and 34.
- **Required change:** Host applies the same secret-shape detector used by the cassette sanitizer, plus a hard length bound, to every provider-authored string (diagnostics, remediation, manual-action text, plan field values) before persisting to state, receipts, doctor output, or support bundles; matches are redacted and flagged as a provider defect diagnostic. Specify in P1; support bundle assembly in P5 re-scans.
- **Acceptance test:** Conformance fixture where the provider returns a diagnostic containing a seeded secret-shaped token: receipt, state, doctor JSON, and support bundle all contain the redaction marker and a provider-defect diagnostic, never the token.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lanes 16, 17, 18

#### ADV-079 — Credential scope qualification is probe-only; no Resend key introspection exists (scenario 16)

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1 (probe operation in handle model, additive), P4 (Resend implementation + doctor)
- **Claim challenged:** Proposal acceptance requires 'management and sending-only scopes are distinguished' and the provider should 'warn/reject unsafe sharing' (provider-plugins.md:1807, 1785).
- **Evidence:** Official list-api-keys returns only id, name, created_at, last_used_at — neither permission nor domain restriction, and never the token (https://resend.com/docs/api-reference/api-keys/list-api-keys); create-api-key documents permission=full_access|sending_access and domain_id restricting only sending_access keys (https://resend.com/docs/api-reference/api-keys/create-api-key). Both key kinds share the re_ prefix (resend.sh:138 regex cannot distinguish them). A sending key on a management endpoint yields restricted_api_key 401 'This API key is restricted to only send emails', while an invalid key yields invalid_api_key 403 (https://resend.com/docs/api-reference/errors) — the 401/403 semantics are inverted relative to common convention. The credential-handle model binds a handle to purpose, origin, methods, and path templates (proposal permission section), with no defined probe operation.
- **Failure or attack sequence:** Two directions. (1) Sending key bound as management credential: first detected only when Resend returns restricted_api_key 401 during Observe; a status-code-based error mapper classifies 401 as 'invalid/expired credential', sending the operator to rotate a perfectly valid key instead of fixing the binding. (2) full_access key bound as the runtime sending credential (exactly what resend.sh:230 does today): every remote signal is success — sending works — so the promised warn/reject has no vendor-side evidence and silently does not fire; a compromised runtime env then holds a key that can delete domains and webhooks. Additionally, any deliberate qualification probe (spend the runtime-purpose handle on GET /domains expecting restricted_api_key) violates the handle's purpose/path binding, so a compliant implementation cannot perform the check the acceptance criterion requires.
- **Impact:** The P4 exit gate 'no powerful management key is projected as the default runtime key' (provider-plugins.md:2147) is unenforceable as specified; misdiagnosed scope errors degrade operator experience; the script-to-shim migration silently preserves an over-privileged runtime key unless this is confronted.
- **Required change:** (a) Add a manifest-declarable, read-only 'scope-qualification probe' operation to the credential-handle model: fixed endpoint (e.g. GET /domains?limit=1), expected-success/expected-restricted semantics, mintable for a runtime-purpose credential under provider.doctor authorization; success ⇒ key is full_access ⇒ warn/reject per environment policy, restricted_api_key ⇒ sending-only confirmed. (b) Require provider error translation keyed on Resend's error name (restricted_api_key vs invalid_api_key), not HTTP status. (c) Document that scope attestation without probing is impossible on Resend.
- **Acceptance test:** Live acceptance with a dedicated team: doctor with a sending_access key bound as management reports 'credential scope insufficient (sending-only)' with rebind remediation; doctor with a full_access key bound as runtime reports the unsafe-sharing warning and production policy rejects projection; cassette replay proves the 401-restricted vs 403-invalid mapping.
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lane 15

#### ADV-080 — Resend CLI local-forwarded mode: tunnel mechanics ambiguous and temp-webhook secret story undefined

- **Severity:** MEDIUM  **Confidence:** MEDIUM  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P4 (Resend), touches P1 exposure-mode documentation
- **Claim challenged:** Proposal: 'Resend's current CLI can register a temporary webhook and forward to a local server' (provider-plugins.md:1789-1790); cross-provider matrix lists 'local forwarder | Stripe CLI | n/a | Resend CLI' (provider-plugins.md:1923) implying Stripe-CLI parity; local-forwarded mode says 'the provider configures the returned signing secret but does not own the long-running process' (provider-plugins.md:1352-1357).
- **Evidence:** Official CLI docs show 'resend webhooks listen --url https://hostname.tailnet-name.ts.net --events email.received' with a user-supplied Tailscale tunnel URL, --forward-to retaining original Svix headers, --port default 4318, and 'registers a temporary webhook, streams events, and cleans up on exit' (https://resend.com/docs/cli); the CLI 2.0 announcement instead says the CLI 'handles the tunnel and forwards incoming events to your local server' (https://resend.com/blog/resend-cli-2). Neither source documents how the temporary webhook's signing secret is exposed for local verification. The starter's receiver hard-requires a signing secret and verifies with Svix (module-saas-starter/module/services/accounts/code/pkg/email/resend_webhook.go:45,50).
- **Failure or attack sequence:** In local-forwarded dogfood, events are signed with the CLI's temporary webhook key (Svix keys are per-endpoint: 'each webhook and its metadata are signed with a unique key specific to the endpoint', https://resend.com/docs/webhooks/verify-webhooks-requests). The projected email@1 webhook secret belongs to the managed public webhook — a different endpoint — so the starter's verifier rejects 100% of forwarded events. Which secret to project in local-forwarded mode, and how to obtain the temp webhook's secret (CLI stdout? API retrieve of a CLI-owned, exit-deleted webhook — racy), is unspecified; and if the docs-page reading is correct, the mode also silently requires a user-managed public tunnel, contradicting the founder-UX premise that Codefly resolves exposure.
- **Impact:** The P4 dogfood paths 'sent, delivered, bounced, complained, duplicated, and out-of-order events are exercised' (provider-plugins.md:1813-1814) cannot pass in local-forwarded mode as designed; risk of shipping a mode that verifies nothing or tempts disabling signature verification locally.
- **Required change:** P4 must live-qualify the CLI: does webhooks listen provision its own tunnel or require one; how is the temporary webhook's signing secret surfaced; does --forward-to preserve verifiable signatures end-to-end. Then define local-forwarded secret resolution explicitly — either capture the temp secret through a supervised companion contract, or drop the Resend CLI from v1 and implement local-forwarded as managed-public-webhook + generic tunnel with the managed secret. Correct the matrix row to note Resend CLI is not transport-equivalent to Stripe CLI until proven.
- **Acceptance test:** Live acceptance: run the qualified local-forwarded flow and assert the starter's Svix verifier accepts forwarded events using only Codefly-projected configuration (no manually pasted secret), and that no temporary webhook remains after CLI exit (complete webhook listing before/after).
- **Lane/reviewer:** Lane 15

#### ADV-081 — HTTP broker reuse (Web toolbox)

- **Severity:** MEDIUM _(reported as HIGH; adjudicated down by the verification pass)_  **Confidence:** HIGH  **Protocol-breaking if deferred:** no  **Affected phase/issues:** P1 (broker), gates P2-P4
- **Verification:** REFUTED — Downgrade to MEDIUM (documentation correction): reword provider-plugins.md:659-661 to stop claiming the Web toolbox supplies 'most' of the guard behavior (it supplies allowlist/redirect/timeout/cap only; the security-critical majority is new per the proposal's own 1424-1445 list), change 'extract' in row 255 to 'reimplement host-side using toolbox-web's allowlist/redirect behavior as reference', and add proxy-env neutralization and IP-literal/rebinding bullets to the 1424 checklist. No phase gating change needed — the broker is already an explicit, requirement-backed P1 deliverable.
- **Claim challenged:** 'The existing Web toolbox supplies most of the HTTP guard behavior. The provider broker adds typed credential injection, operation-aware method policy, and cassette integration' (provider-plugins.md:658-662); reuse row 'Web toolbox … extract a host-mediated provider HTTP broker' (provider-plugins.md:255).
- **Evidence:** toolbox-web/server.go is the complete request path (~285 lines). Present: exact-hostname allowlist (server.go:207-210), redirect hostname recheck (server.go:76-85), 30s timeout and 4MiB cap (server.go:21-26, 251-260).
