Full lane narratives are reproduced below.

### Lane narratives

#### product-abstraction
##### Lane 1: product need and abstraction choice

The product problem is real but is mis-stated, and the proposed remedy is sized for a requirement the proposal never declares. What is genuinely broken today is credential hygiene and lifecycle state, not port-copying or founder ergonomics: the current scripts already resolve dynamic ports via `codefly endpoint` (module-saas-starter/scripts/setup/provider-common.sh:104-114), already do hidden prompts, Git-ignore assertions, 0600 atomic installs, and doctor runs — but they provably project management/setup credentials into application runtime (sentry.sh:203 writes the setup SENTRY_AUTH_TOKEN into error-tracking.secret.env; stripe.sh:219 writes the management-capable sk_test key as the runtime STRIPE_API_KEY; workos.sh:347-349 duplicates one key as both client secret and management key), keep no record of created remote resources (stripe.sh:196 captures the webhook secret but never persists the endpoint ID), and cannot re-plan, import, or detect drift. So a first-class lifecycle IS justified — but by secret hygiene and the production path, not by local founder UX, which improves only marginally in v1 (the Stripe local-forwarded mode still requires a manually run Stripe CLI and a pasted whsec via 'safe host input', provider-plugins.md:1624-1629, 1370-1373). 'Provider' is an acceptable name with the normative glossary; the mental model diverges from Terraform (binding+projection instance, not resource-type factory), which is fine. Environment binding is the right ownership scope: environments already own ingress and secret backends (core/resources/environment.go:78,134) and multiple services consume one projected capability. Sharing one protocol between dogfood and production is correct given production admission gates, and observe-only plus managed modes belong in one abstraction (Terraform data-source/resource and Pulumi Read precedent; Sentry v0.1 validates the observe-only leg, provider-plugins.md:1697-1701). The two serious Lane-1 problems: (a) the system is simultaneously too broad and too narrow — the entire v1 managed inventory is two webhook endpoints (cross-provider matrix, provider-plugins.md:1918) while the protocol freezes IMPORT/REPLACE/DELETE/UpgradeState/ownership semantics against them, yet 5 of the 8 existing starter integrations (WorkOS, PostHog, Turnstile, OTLP, plus the largest script, workos.sh at 377 lines) stay on scripts through P4, so scripts are not meaningfully replaced in the funded roadmap; (b) the untrusted-binary apparatus (new agent kind, sandbox, UDS auth, manifest ceiling, HTTP broker, credential handles, declarative capture, conformance CI) is justified by 'provider binaries may be compromised', but every provider named anywhere in the proposal is first-party codefly.dev-published; the Rejected Alternatives section (provider-plugins.md:2293-2340) never evaluates the obvious middle option — a trusted in-host engine with providers as reviewed first-party code or declarative descriptors — which delivers the same lifecycle, projection, plan/state/receipt semantics with a fraction of the machinery (see 'special'). Verdict for this lane: keep the lifecycle (bindings, modes, Validate/Observe/Plan/Apply/Project, state, projection contracts), but P0 must explicitly decide whether third-party provider publication is a real requirement, because that single decision determines whether the sandbox/broker/handle layers are foundation work or deferrable hardening.

##### Lane 23: future-provider pressure

Pressure-testing the protocol against the 12 listed future providers finds four assumptions that break immediately and several that hold. BREAKS: (1) Manifest-constant exact-origin ceilings — the broker 'derives exact origin ceilings from the packaged manifest' (provider-plugins.md:649, 707-708, 1405), but user-supplied API origins are already v1 inputs: Sentry 'API base or regional origin' (1673) and P3 'regional/self-hosted API base handling' (2095), Stripe API base input (1583), Resend API base (1739); OTLP (arbitrary endpoint, otel.sh:110-112) and self-hosted PostHog (posthog.sh capture/api host inputs) make this terminal. This is an internal contradiction that hits in v1, not just the future. (2) Static header-placement credential injection — the handle binds 'allowed origin and authorization/header placement' (475-477) while 'provider-specific signature algorithms remain in the provider agent' (1443-1444); request-signing schemes (SigV4-style object storage, some managed-Postgres control planes) require the raw secret to compute the signature, so either the broker learns vendor signing (contradicting 1443) or the provider gets raw bytes (contradicting the handle model). Inference at MEDIUM confidence pending AWS/Cloudflare doc verification, but the textual contradiction is proven. (3) Pre-existing resolvable credentials — the enumerated secret sources (config, op:// reference, file, hidden prompt, CI channel; 869-877) exclude interactive OAuth consent acquisition and token refresh; an OAuth-only management API (Slack/Google-style) cannot be onboarded and refresh is a credential-mutating operation absent from the action vocabulary. (4) Projection-as-env-vars only — a managed-Postgres or object-storage provider produces a service-graph endpoint/dependency, which the projection contract cannot publish (providers have 'no application endpoint'); the proposal itself defers this boundary (2185-2187) and the correct answer is 'not a provider', which should be stated. HOLDS/PRESSURED: WorkOS fits via MANUAL_ACTION and local-direct exposure, but its only redirect-URI verification signal today is HTML scraping of the authorize page (workos.sh:303-328), which fits neither canonical JSON observation nor JSON-pointer capture — observation must admit 'probe checks' or push them into Doctor. Turnstile/feature-flag/GraphQL-style POST-reads mean host-verifiable read-only-ness degenerates to manifest declaration (method is not a mutation proxy); the per-path method policy can express it but the invariant 'Observe cannot mutate' becomes trust-the-manifest. Multi-region origin discovery (Sentry region URLs) works because plan digests bind resolved origins, provided finding (1) is fixed. Async provisioning survives via checkpoints but strains one-shot Apply with short-TTL scoped auth; non-retrievable secrets are the design's core case and hold; no-management-API providers (Turnstile, OTLP) reduce to validate+project, which works but demonstrates that the heavy plugin apparatus is unused for the long tail — reinforcing the Lane 1 sizing concern. Net: v0 must freeze an origin-admission mechanism and reserve an extensible auth-scheme field in the request descriptor/credential handle; everything else can remain capability-negotiated.

#### reuse-audit
##### Lane 2: Codefly reuse audit (non-policy rows)

The proposal's reuse table is broadly honest about what must be built new (writable secret sink, projection writer, provider advertisement helper, provider state schema), and several load-bearing primitives are genuinely production-wired and reusable as-is: the authenticated per-spawn UDS loader with 256-bit bearer tokens, handshake version checks, private per-spawn socket dirs, stale-socket sweep, sandbox wrap, principal binding, and production admission (core/agents/manager/loader.go); structured failures (core/proto/codefly/base/v0/failure.proto); read-only configuration loading with op:// secret-reference resolution, caching, and redaction (core/configurations/secrets.go, core/resources/configuration.go:169-178); the Ed25519 receipt library and UNCERTAIN/COMPENSATED execution stages (core/executionreceipt/receipt.go, core/proto/codefly/execution/v1/execution.proto); the doctor JSON contract with stable codes (cli/cmd/doctor_workspace.go). However, four rows materially overstate current capability. (1) 'Web toolbox supplies most of the HTTP guard behavior' is wrong: toolbox-web/server.go is ~285 lines whose guards (exact-hostname allowlist, redirect hostname recheck, 30s timeout, 4MiB cap) execute inside the untrusted plugin process, pass caller-supplied Authorization headers straight through, honor proxy environment variables via the default transport, and have no method/path policy, credential injection, idempotency, rate-limit normalization, or cassettes — the broker is NEW security-critical host-side code. (2) Cassette testing does not exist in code anywhere (go-vcr appears only as an unused cli/go.sum entry). (3) Execution receipts are wired only behind the gateway daemon's opt-in --governed-execution flag (cli/cmd/daemon_execution.go), so 'every provider mutation produces signed receipts' needs new producer/attestor/key wiring for ordinary CLI commands. (4) Agent kind routing is not one switch to extend but at least four duplicated, mutually inconsistent switches (resources.Agent.Path has toolboxes; manager.FindLocalLatest silently defaults unknown kinds to modules/; cli/cmd/agents/build.go defaults to services/; the GitHub release repo name is hardcoded service-<name> regardless of kind), and the GitHub auto-download fallback in manager.Load performs no digest verification. Finally, the macOS sandbox backend is allow-default on reads by explicit design, so the claimed 'no workspace reads / secrets unreachable' provider guarantee is unenforceable on the primary dogfood OS without new read-deny rules. None of this breaks the protocol shape, but P1 sizing and the security claims must be corrected before issues are cut.

#### agentkind-protocol
##### Lane 3: agent-kind and packaging architecture

Adding PROVIDER=6 to base.v0.Agent.Kind is enum-compatible (TOOLBOX=5 is the last value, core/proto/codefly/base/v0/agent.proto:9-27) and the spawn/transport layer is genuinely kind-agnostic and reusable as-is: manager.Load resolves the binary via Agent.Path(), spawns over a private per-spawn UDS with a bearer token (core/agents/manager/loader.go:637, 738-775; sockets.go:12-48), and the toolbox launch wrapper (core/toolbox/launch/launch.go:99-173) is a working precedent for a typed leaf plugin with sandbox, principal, and production admission. However, the proposal's single reuse-table row 'add PROVIDER / codefly:provider routing' materially understates the work: kind routing is not a registry but at least 20 independent switch/default sites across core and cli with three DIFFERENT silent fallback behaviors (build defaults unknown kinds to services/, versions inventory defaults to modules/, FindLocalLatest defaults to modules/, Path() fails closed), and the most recently added kind (TOOLBOX) is demonstrably mis-routed in production today: this host's toolbox binaries (web__*, nix__*) sit under ~/.codefly/agents/services/codefly.dev because cli/cmd/agents/build.go:514-519 routes them there, while resources.Agent.Path() (core/resources/agent.go:168-169) and versions.go:500-501 expect agents/toolboxes/, and there is no toolboxes/ directory on disk at all. Publish (cli/cmd/publish/agent_release.go:400-421) and agent ci (cli/cmd/agents/ci.go:427-428) hard-reject every non-service/module kind. Beyond routing, two packaging gaps are more serious: (1) the security-load-bearing packaged manifest (provider.codefly.yaml) has NO distribution channel in any existing artifact format — GitHub releases are a tar.gz containing a bare binary, OCI is a single binary blob, and Nix produces bin/service-{name}; toolbox manifests exist only in source checkouts (LoadToolboxFromDir, core/resources/toolbox.go:108-131), so the manifest-ceiling model is unenforceable for installed providers; (2) the GitHub download path performs no digest or signature verification whatsoever (core/agents/manager/downloader.go:59-70 is http.Get + extract; only OCI verifies a blob sha256, store.go:157-189), and release naming hardcodes service- for repo, asset, OCI layer, and Nix bin path, while proposed repos are named provider-stripe. The JOB kind precedent (constant defined in core/resources/job.go:17-18, never RegisterAgent'ed, no Path() branch) proves partial kind additions ship silently. Uninstall does not exist as a command, so 'uninstall with active bindings' is entirely new design. Conclusion: the kind is the right mechanism, but P1 must first centralize kind→{subdir, repo prefix, asset name, publish/CI semantics} into the RegisterAgent table with fail-closed defaults, and must define manifest-in-artifact packaging with digest binding — neither is optional routing work.

##### Lane 4: protocol coherence and evolvability

The phase model is fundamentally sound: no long-lived Configure session (each request carries ProviderContext) is the right call and matches both the restart-safe toolbox leaf precedent (toolbox.proto's stateless RPC surface) and Terraform/Pulumi lessons; Observe/Plan separation with offline deterministic Plan is cleanly achievable for all three initial providers (Stripe: diff desired webhook/events against observed endpoint list; Sentry: pure observe/project; Resend: domains+webhooks), and UNCERTAIN outcomes map onto the existing execution receipt stage (core/proto/codefly/execution/v1/execution.proto:22-23) and structured failures (base/v0/failure.proto:150-170 with retryable/retry_after). But the protocol section as written cannot be frozen, because it omits its own largest surface: the provider→host HTTP-broker request-descriptor channel, the checkpoint channel, and the output-commit channel are all host-side services the provider must call, none has a proto, a version, or negotiation rules — the document's 7-RPC table covers only the host→provider direction, yet Observe and Apply are unimplementable without the reverse direction, and the manifest's method/path/secret-capture declarations must syntactically match a descriptor schema that does not exist. Second, Apply's orchestration weight is wrong: a single Apply RPC in which the provider executes the whole plan and 'emits checkpoints' cannot satisfy invariant 25 (every externally visible effect durably checkpointed before the next mutation) or per-action revocation checks unless the transport is streaming-with-acks or, better, host-driven per-action execution — the simpler host-driven shape also converts 'provider must reject unplanned actions' from a promise into a construction. Third, approval stability is broken by design: the plan digest binds the observed-state digest while canonical observation is required to include volatile fields (rate-limit status, retry-after, request IDs, observation time), so every refresh invalidates approval. Remaining gaps: output placement is ambiguous (which RPC carries final typed outputs; whether observe-only bindings run a projection-only Apply), Doctor's remote half duplicates Observe, state versioning lacks a minimum-upgradable-from range, downgrade rules, and an opaque provider-data field for unknown-field survival, pagination/listing completeness is unmodeled although uniqueness decisions depend on it, RPC size bounds are unstated, and the document is internally inconsistent about whether the protocol is v0 or 1. None of this requires abandoning the shape; all of it must be resolved before v0 freeze, and the freeze/negotiate split is enumerated in the special artifact.

#### permissions
##### Lane 5 — permission, principal, and approval architecture

The core/policy package is a genuinely strong, well-factored authority layer: Principal (principal.go) is validated/immutable and cannot be impersonated across the callback because manager.Load binds the spawn-time principal as the trusted subject (loader.go:836, callback.go:120-135, 262-274); scoped authorization binds action/resource/audience/CatalogDigest/RequestDigest/TTL/MaxUses with exact-match verification and fail-closed unknown-caveat handling (scoped_auth.go, scoped_auth_v2.go:311-374); SaasPDP fails closed on backend error and never caches denies (pdp_saas.go:148-211, 297-311); CeilingPDP enforces the manifest as a maximum (pdp_ceiling.go). HOWEVER, the proposal's central claim — 'reuse Codefly's existing authorization stack end to end' — is not supported by the CLI that would run `codefly provider ...`. The only production wiring of principal/PDP/scoped-auth is (a) core/toolbox/session (used by the conformance harness and Mind) and (b) cli/pkg/gateway prepared-mutation (Mind's daemon, VerifyEd25519). The codefly CLI's own agent supervisor loads agents with WithoutPrincipal()+WithoutSandbox() (cli/pkg/engine/supervisor.go:142-143) and no cli/ code constructs a policy.Principal or a SaasPDP. So from the CLI host's perspective the entire stack is PARTIAL/UNWIRED and the 'foundation' issue must build the whole host-side authorization path, not compose an existing one. Three structural gaps make the proposal's per-plan-action model NOT achievable by reuse: (1) the permission callback trusts the plugin's action/resource claims (only principal is overridden), so a provider that self-authorizes each brokered request could under-declare the action — the host must DERIVE action/resource from the approved plan and the actual HTTP request, which is new logic; (2) there is no per-outbound-HTTP-request authorization primitive — GatewayEvaluator mints one token per RPC/CallTool, not one per broker request, so 're-check at every brokered sub-operation' (proposal:442) and revocation-mid-Apply are new; (3) no PDP in core emits RequireApproval and escalation is an agent-SDK/saas-starter flow, so the human-CLI approval binding the proposal describes for provider.create/delete does not exist yet. The canonical action vocabulary is reasonable and the resource ordering (remote-id last) is compatible with the trailing-`*` matcher, but the matcher cannot express mid-path wildcards (permissions.go:415-497) and the manifest examples use the broadest `provider:stripe/*` form, which prefix-matches every workspace/environment/binding/type/remote-id — the ambient-CRUD path. Net: the primitives are the right ones and should be reused, but the proposal must be corrected to state that almost all of the provider authorization host is NEW work built ON these primitives, and must add plan-derived authorization, per-broker-request re-checks, and a CLI-host approval path.

##### Lane 2 (policy/permission rows) — reuse classification

policy.Principal + manager.WithPrincipal + WithProductionAdmission are REUSE AS-IS as types/enforcement but PARTIAL/UNWIRED from the CLI (no CLI code binds a real principal; supervisor uses WithoutPrincipal). PermissionPolicy + NewCeilingPDP are REUSE AS-IS for the manifest-ceiling concept but the glob matcher only supports exact/bare-`*`/trailing-`prefix*` (permissions.go:489-498), a REUSE WITH SMALL EXTENSION if richer resource scoping is ever needed. GatewayEvaluator + ScopedAuthorization (v1 HMAC + v2 Ed25519, CatalogDigest/RequestDigest/MaxUses/caveats) are REUSE AS-IS and are the correct approval-binding substrate; v2 Ed25519 is already production-wired in cli/pkg/gateway prepared_mutation.go. The permissions callback (WithPermissionsCallback / PermissionsCallbackServer) is REUSE AS-IS for principal binding but DO NOT REUSE for per-plan-action authorization because it trusts plugin-supplied action/resource (callback.go:286-298). Escalation (RequireApproval + EscalationGrantor) is PARTIAL/UNWIRED: defined in core and implemented by saas-starter host, but no core PDP emits RequireApproval and there is no human-CLI escalation path. SaasPDP cache/revocation/fail-closed is REUSE AS-IS (pdp_saas.go). Policy observability (PDPMetrics/RecordDecision) is REUSE AS-IS. Break-glass, AllowAllPDP, PDPModeOff, WithoutPrincipal are the documented bypasses the proposal (invariant 10) says provider production admission must reject and keep greppable — that rejection is NEW; WithProductionAdmission validates sandbox/principal/callback/secret (loader.go:609-632) but does NOT check the break-glass env var or PDP mode.

#### sandbox-broker
##### Lane 6: sandbox and process isolation

The claim that a compromised provider can reach neither an unapproved origin nor workspace/secrets is refuted on macOS, partially true on Linux, and in both cases enforced by code that is not wired into any non-toolbox agent path today. The only sandbox is core/runners/sandbox (bwrap on Linux, sandbox-exec on macOS), whose own docs state it 'is NOT a defense against a hostile binary that's actively trying to escape' (core/runners/sandbox/doc.go:27-32) - it explicitly disclaims the threat model the proposal assigns it (proposal lines 1385-1398, invariants 11/12/13). The macOS profile starts with '(allow default)' and denies only file-write plus four home subdirectories (core/runners/sandbox/sandboxexec.go:100-124), so reads are unconstrained: the workspace, every projected *.secret.env (proposal line 941) and all of ~/.codefly (core/resources/global.go:57) - the proposal's provider state root (line 1248) - are readable by every sandboxed agent, and the deny entry '.config/codefly/secrets' points at a path nothing in the repo uses. On Linux bwrap does confine reads to declared --ro-bind paths plus /usr,/lib,/bin,/sbin,/etc (core/runners/sandbox/bwrap.go:107-131), materially stronger, but omits --unshare-pid/--unshare-ipc while mounting '--proc /proc' in the host PID namespace, so the provider can read /proc/<pid>/environ of same-UID processes and signal them. Both backends inherit the full parent environment: loader sets cmd.Env = os.Environ() (core/agents/manager/loader.go:722) with no --clearenv, so every provider gets the operator's whole environment (1Password/cloud/vendor tokens) plus CODEFLY_AGENT_TOKEN, CODEFLY_PRINCIPAL_TOKEN and CODEFLY_SCOPED_AUTHZ_SECRET (loader.go:777, 800-806, 817), defeating control #7 before any credential handle exists. Capacity is self-declared: policy.SandboxPolicy comes from the plugin manifest and is applied verbatim (core/policy/permissions.go:174-206, core/toolbox/launch/launch.go:199-235); nothing imposes a per-kind ceiling, and production admission only requires a non-native backend (loader.go:611-615) plus a non-empty policy (core/resources/toolbox.go:186-197) - toolbox-web proves the consequence by declaring 'network: open' (toolbox.codefly.yaml:26) and asserting in its own test that this manifest passes ValidateForProduction (manifest_test.go:13-17). So invariant 11 has no enforcement point. The sandbox is also toolbox-only: every other agent load path passes WithoutSandbox (core/services/agent.go:148, cli/pkg/engine/supervisor.go:142, core/sdk/sdk.go:168), and UDS is opt-in (loader.go:297,416) with one caller (core/toolbox/session/session.go:276) - which matters because --unshare-net makes the default TCP-loopback handshake impossible on Linux, so a provider host must mandate WithUDS. Genuine strengths: per-spawn UDS dirs are 0700 with crash sweeping (core/agents/manager/sockets.go:34-65); the callback socket is 0600 with the spawn-time principal bound as authoritative subject (core/policy/callback.go:161-172, loader.go:836-843) so a provider cannot claim another principal; confinement is inherited by children (TestE2E_OSSandbox_InheritedByChildProcesses); agents get their own pgid with pgid-file tracking; and under an enforcing sandbox one provider cannot reach another's socket or state (bwrap's fresh /tmp tmpfs; the narrow macOS unix-socket regex). Net: isolation is real but OS-asymmetric, weaker than claimed, and unproven on the primary dogfood platform - CI is ubuntu-latest only (.github/workflows/go.yml:23) and the darwin loopback e2e is darwin-tagged, so the Seatbelt profile never runs in CI.

##### Lane 7: HTTP broker design

A host-mediated broker is the right direction for these three vendors - Stripe, Sentry and Resend are plain REST/JSON (Sentry documents JSON bodies under /api/0/; Stripe is form-encoded), so no provider-native SDK is needed and v0.1 needs no streaming or multipart - but the claim that 'the existing Web toolbox supplies most of the HTTP guard behavior' (proposal 660-661, 1424-1426) is false where it matters: toolbox-web's guard runs inside the untrusted plugin process, its allowlist arrives via that process's own env var (toolbox-web/cmd/toolbox-web/main.go:22-24), and its manifest grants network: open (toolbox.codefly.yaml:26). An in-process allowlist in a process with raw egress is a comment, not a control. Recommendation: do not extract toolbox-web - there is nothing substantial to extract (host-map lookup at server.go:207-210, a CheckRedirect closure at 76-85, a LimitReader at 251-260). Build the broker as new code in core (direction is already correct: toolbox-web requires core v0.2.23, go.mod:8; core does not depend on toolbox-web), then re-point toolbox-web at it so the toolbox becomes a request descriptor rather than an egress holder. Attacking the proposed broker as a confused deputy exposes gaps the proposal does not close. (1) Authorization binding: the normative manifest declares only api-origins with no method/path/query/body constraints (695-742) while the requirements assume manifest-derived method/path patterns (1437); and method+path is insufficient anyway - Stripe update is POST /v1/webhook_endpoints/:id (docs.stripe.com/api/webhook_endpoints), so create/update/retrieve/delete differ only by :id presence and value, and Sentry 'sometimes' passes parameters in the querystring even for POST/PUT/DELETE (docs.sentry.io/api/requests/). The broker must bind {id} to the approved plan action's remote ID and constrain query/body, or a provider.update approval for endpoint A authorizes mutating endpoint B. (2) provider.observe is rated low risk (line 393) yet an origin-only ceiling with a management key permits unlimited GETs over customers/invoices/charges. (3) Origin ceiling versus operator-supplied base URL is unresolved: Sentry regional hosts (us.sentry.io, de.sentry.io) and self-hosted installs plus a STRIPE_API_BASE input (line 860) cannot be enumerated at packaging time, and once operator-supplied origins are allowed the broker - inside the unsandboxed host process - becomes an SSRF instrument against loopback, 169.254.169.254, RFC1918 and VPN space, with DNS rebinding available because the hostname is validated once and re-resolved at dial time. (4) Header policy must be an allowlist, not the promised denylist: Go turns URL userinfo into Authorization Basic when no Authorization header is set (GOROOT/src/net/http/client.go:251-257), and Idempotency-Key, Host, Accept-Encoding, Transfer-Encoding, Expect, X-Forwarded-* and Proxy-* must all be host-owned. (5) Compression is a capture bypass: Go does not decompress when the caller set Accept-Encoding (GOROOT/src/net/http/transport.go:195-202), so a provider-set Accept-Encoding: gzip either defeats JSON-Pointer capture or lets the raw secret-bearing body through, and also breaks byte-cap accounting. (6) Proxy: a client built like the precedent (nil Transport -> DefaultTransport, Proxy: ProxyFromEnvironment - transport.go:46-57, 491-506) silently honors HTTP_PROXY/HTTPS_PROXY from the host environment, which the allowlist never validates; combined with the custom-CA support self-hosted Sentry needs, that is credential disclosure. (7) Size policy: truncating and flagging truncated:true (server.go:251-260) is exactly wrong for a broker whose response may carry a write-once secret - truncation after a successful mutation destroys the only recoverable copy; the broker needs small per-operation caps with hard failure plus header-size, redirect-count and JSON depth/duplicate-key/value-length limits. (8) Redirects: Go's own rule (Authorization forwarded only on exact/subdomain match, pkg.go.dev/net/http Client.Do) helps, but a broker re-injecting credentials per hop loses it; credentialed requests should not follow redirects at all. (9) Retries: Stripe prunes idempotency keys after >=24h and 'generate[s] a new request if a key is reused after the original is pruned' (docs.stripe.com/api/idempotent_requests), so no generic retry layer may cross that window; and cassette determinism is threatened by host-injected Idempotency-Key values derived from operation/attempt IDs, which the request matcher must normalize. No cassette infrastructure exists in core or cli today (searching for 'cassette' returns documentation only), so that reuse row is new implementation.

#### secrets
##### Lane 8: credentials and secret lifecycle

The proposal's host-mediated boundary (resolver -> handle -> broker injection -> sink -> projection) is the correct shape, and several claims verified: the 1Password resolver is genuinely read-only (core/configurations/secrets.go:137-144 runs only `op read`; failure output is suppressed at secrets.go:150-165), resolved values never touch disk in the loader path, ScopedAuthorization already carries AudienceID/CatalogDigest/RequestDigest/MaxUses/Caveats (core/policy/scoped_auth_v2.go:99-122,337-369) so credential handles can be a genuine application of the existing token system, and Ed25519-signed receipts exist (core/executionreceipt/receipt.go:76-100). However, three lifecycle boundaries are weaker than claimed. First, every agent spawned via the manager inherits the full CLI environment (core/agents/manager/loader.go:722 `cmd.Env = os.Environ()`), so user-exported vendor secrets (the exact workflow today's scripts support, e.g. module-saas-starter/scripts/setup/stripe.sh:22 reads STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET from env), OP_* 1Password session/service tokens, and proxy variables all reach the provider binary unless a scrubbed spawn path is added — the proposal never requires this. Second, purpose separation is unenforceable downstream of projection: ConfigurationValue carries only key/value/secret (core/proto/codefly/base/v0/configuration.proto:8-15), so purpose/browser/provenance metadata is discarded at the stated conversion step and any service consuming a configuration receives all its secret values as process env (core/resources/environment_variables_manager.go:490-512; native runner cmd.Env core/runners/base/native_runner.go:315-323; docker container env with only log-side redaction core/runners/dockerrun/docker_runner.go:297-315). Third, secret minimization is contradicted by the existing loader, which resolves every secret value in a loaded configuration in one pass with a per-load cache (core/configurations/secrets.go:232-255,202-216) — management and runtime keys are both materialized in host memory even for observe-only operations; selective resolution is unacknowledged new work. The sink contract also lacks idempotent addressing/lookup needed for orphan recovery, fingerprints are undefined (should be keyed), and rotation/leak-recovery workflows are absent. None of these force a redesign of the trust boundary itself; they are named, resolvable gaps — but the env-inheritance one must be a hard production-admission gate.

##### Lane 9: one-time secret capture

Declarative JSON Pointer capture as specified is not robust and needs a bounded redesign before P1. The mechanism is scoped to 'successful API responses that return a secret only once' — i.e., mutation responses with an approved plan and a prepared sink — and uses a single fixed JSON Pointer per declaration. Two of the three launch vendors return secrets on read paths where no plan and no sink exist: Resend states 'calls to create, retrieve, or list webhooks will also return the signing secret in the response body' (https://resend.com/docs/webhooks/verify-webhooks-requests), and Sentry's client-key list response contains `secret` and `dsn.secret` fields alongside the public DSN (https://docs.sentry.io/api/projects/list-a-projects-client-keys/). Under the proposal as written, those raw secrets flow through the broker into the provider process during Observe, directly contradicting the proposal's own sentence that raw secret-bearing responses must not reach the provider process, and list responses are arrays that a fixed pointer like /secret cannot address (/data/*/signing_secret). Separately, the miss path is not fail-closed: capture matches method+path+content-type, so a hostile or drifting API that returns a different content type, malformed JSON, duplicate keys, or a relocated field simply fails to match and the raw body is forwarded unredacted. The good parts are real: parse-before-log ordering is specified, sink identity is bound into the plan digest, sink preparation is required before the request is sent, the no-rollback rule for persisted one-time secrets is explicitly correct, and JSON-only capture is sufficient for the Stripe/Sentry/Resend cohort (all JSON responses). Recommended replacement: keep declarative capture but (a) generalize declarations to read operations with array-wildcard pointers and a sink-less 'suppress' disposition so Observe responses are redacted; (b) make capture-rule match fail closed — if method+path matches a rule and the response cannot be parsed and captured as declared, block the response entirely; (c) require the provider to always receive a re-serialized canonical JSON body (never original raw bytes), which also closes duplicate-key and raw-body side channels; (d) drive the cassette sanitizer from the same declared secret-field list rather than heuristics alone. With those four changes the declarative approach is sound; without them it fails on the launch cohort itself.

#### state-recovery
##### Lane 10: state, locking, ownership, and drift

The proposal's honest modeling of partial/uncertain outcomes is its strongest state-related property, and the claimed reuse of Codefly's execution journal is genuine and high quality: cli/pkg/executionjournal/journal.go is a real bbolt-backed, fsync-durable, owner-only, symlink-rejecting store that already enforces terminal-requires-STARTED ordering (journal.go:219-224), immutable attempt bindings (journal.go:225-232), one-terminal-stage-per-attempt (journal.go:205-211), operation-vs-attempt identity matching the proposal's requirement (journal.go:743-775, execution.proto:49-52), an integrity check on open (journal.go:142-151), and an IncompleteAttempts crash-recovery query designed exactly for the append-UNCERTAIN-on-restart pattern (journal.go:432-470). However, five material gaps remain. First, the proposed state address inherits executionruntime.DefaultStateDir's Abs+Clean-only canonicalization (cli/pkg/executionruntime/runtime.go:62-78, no EvalSymlinks), and Workspace has no stable identity beyond a name (core/resources/workspace.go:40-75), so a moved or symlink-accessed workspace silently orphans all ownership state with no specified detection or migration. Second, per-binding state directories (provider-plugins.md:1248) make the stated invariant that one remote object cannot be bound to multiple local addresses 'in the same state backend' (provider-plugins.md:1294-1295) structurally unenforceable across bindings and environments. Third, the lock is specified only as 'an inter-process lock' (provider-plugins.md:1252) with no scope, hold-duration, or apply-start CAS-under-lock semantics, and bbolt's exclusive per-open file lock makes naive held-open designs block even read-only doctor/observe. Fourth, lost-response recovery for secret-bearing creations is caught between 'refuses an automatic retry' (provider-plugins.md:1213-1214) and reliance on idempotency keys (provider-plugins.md:1224-1226), with no same-attempt replay policy and no ownership stamping of the host-generated prospective resource ID into vendor metadata, so a dropped Stripe response degenerates to manual import plus destructive REPLACE. Fifth, downgrade (older agent reading newer state) is unspecified even though both version signals exist (provider-plugins.md:1092, 1264), and provider receipts reuse silently depends on a Work Context authority that local dogfood may not have (cli/pkg/executionrecorder/recorder.go:123, execution.proto:62). Drift handling (provider-owned canonical fields, provider-plugins.md:1266) and deletion protection (retain default, exact-ownership destroy) are directionally sound; the deletion-with-active-bindings protection is only a 'should be blocked or loudly diagnosed' aspiration with no inventory mechanism. UNCERTAIN plus next-plan reconciliation is sufficient for most crash windows once the recovery sweep is wired; manual intervention remains required for workspace moves, state deletion, cross-machine shared accounts, and lost one-time secrets.

#### config-endpoints
##### Lane 11: configuration projections and contracts

The generic-contract direction is correct and already proven in miniature: the six-integrations starter ships billing/email/error-tracking as plain named env configurations (module-saas-starter/configurations/local-dogfood/*.env.example) that services consume by name via workspace-configuration-dependencies (core/resources/service.go:57) with injection at cli/pkg/orchestration/runner.go:273, so provider projection through the same files does NOT bypass Codefly SDK configuration injection — verified. The proposal is also honest that the loader is read-only (core/configurations/ contains only read+resolve; the sole secret backend is the read-only 1Password `op read` resolver, core/configurations/secrets.go:126-144), so the atomic writer and writable sink are genuinely new machinery. However, the contract layer is materially under-specified in ways with concrete failures: (1) the rich PUBLIC/SENSITIVE/SECRET/browser-exposable/purpose classification collapses into the single ConfigurationValue.secret bool (core/proto/codefly/base/v0/configuration.proto:8-15) with no proto extension proposed; (2) purpose separation is impossible at the only existing consumer granularity — the whole configuration name — and the proposal's own Sentry example violates its own invariant by putting the build token in the same `error-tracking` configuration the frontend runtime consumes; (3) `billing@1` versioning has no schema artifact, no owner, and no consumer-side declaration, so 'compatibility/version negotiation' is currently unenforceable; (4) the promised 'atomically rename the complete output set' is not implementable for a multi-file set inside the shared profile directory; (5) reference-only secret files (`*.secret.ref.env`) hard-reject plaintext (core/configurations/secrets.go:66-78), which the projection examples ignore. Disconnect-vs-destroy is cleanly separated on paper but ignores running consumers that hold injected values until restart. None of this invalidates the direction; all of it must be resolved before P2 because the contract IDs are baked into manifests and plan digests.

##### Lane 12: endpoints, ingress, and local forwarding

The semantic endpoint-reference design (service/endpoint/path, never a URL or port) is the right call and is implementable on existing machinery: local resolution is a deterministic SHA-256 port hash (core/network/port.go:117-132 ToNamedPort via network.NativeFor, core/network/runtime_manager.go:55-74), stable across restarts, and no element of the proposal requires hardcoding a generated port — the current scripts already derive the origin via `codefly endpoint` (provider-common.sh:104-114). The four exposure modes are a sound taxonomy and the WorkOS local-direct counterexample holds. Three real gaps: (1) the claimed reuse of Environment.Ingress for public-HTTPS-origin resolution is overstated — Ingress is YAML data consumed only by orchestration cloning and gitops k8s rendering (core/resources/environment.go:79-84,134; cli/pkg/gitops/module_bundle.go:142,166); no (env, service, endpoint) → public origin resolver exists anywhere, and the scripts prove it by requiring a hand-passed --webhook-origin (provider-common.sh:123-127); this is NEW P1 work, and Stripe requires registered endpoints be 'publicly accessible HTTPS URLs' (docs.stripe.com/webhooks), so ingress-before-Apply validation must exist before any `public`-mode mutation. (2) Static hash resolution silently diverges from the flow-authoritative bound port for container runtimes and scoped flows (cli/cmd/common/endpoints.go:100-124; namingScope folds into the hash at runtime_manager.go:68-72), and the proposal never says which resolution is authoritative for provider operations — a wrong-port webhook registration path. (3) Forwarders (Stripe/Resend CLIs) are session-scoped companions with no principal, supervision, or receipts; Resend's CLI registers a temporary webhook and cleans up on exit (resend.com/docs/cli), so any projected webhook secret goes stale every restart, and CI-without-ingress rests entirely on these unsupervised processes — deferring companion ownership is defensible only if P2/P4 acceptance states the stale-secret and restart story explicitly. Webhook-before-ready races are adequately absorbed by vendor retries (Stripe test mode: three retries over a few hours) plus the starter's durable receiver, but only within that bounded retry budget.

#### stripe
##### Lane 13: Stripe reference design

The Stripe v0.1 design is substantially confirmed against current official docs: webhook-endpoint CRUD exists exactly as assumed (POST /v1/webhook_endpoints create/update, GET retrieve/list, DELETE), the signing secret is 'Only returned at creation' via the API (docs.stripe.com/api/webhook_endpoints/object), idempotency keys apply to all POSTs with replay of the original status+body and pruning 'after they're at least 24 hours old' (docs.stripe.com/api/idempotent_requests), per-request Stripe-Version pinning is real, and endpoint-level api_version overrides the account default for event payloads (docs.stripe.com/api/versioning). The Stripe CLI 'listen --forward-to' forwarding model matches the proposal's local-forwarded mode: it does not create a remote endpoint and its whsec is CLI-specific, distinct from any registered endpoint secret. Two material gaps survive falsification attempts: (1) the proposal's `stripe.account` observed resource claims to observe 'sandbox/live mode', but the Stripe Account object has no livemode attribute and the provider never sees the raw key prefix (it holds only an opaque credential handle), so the load-bearing local-live guard has no specified detection mechanism — it must be explicitly assigned to the host at credential-resolution time (key-prefix classification of sk_/rk_ test vs live) plus livemode cross-checks on brokered responses; (2) webhook api_version is create-only and cannot be updated, so changing the pinned API version on a managed endpoint is a REPLACE that mints a new signing secret, which the Stripe section's declared capability set (create/update/import/delete, no replace) and the human plan example ('STRIPE_API_VERSION: 2025-… → 2026-02-25.clover' with 'secret: unchanged') both get wrong. Secondary confirmed facts the design should absorb: idempotent replay within the retention window re-returns the original body including the secret (a safe lost-response recovery path the proposal does not exploit), the Dashboard can reveal and roll endpoint secrets (so 'secret unrecoverable' is API-only, and MANUAL_ACTION recovery exists), webhook endpoints support metadata (usable as the Codefly ownership stamp; today's script sets none), accounts are capped at 16 webhook endpoints, and restricted keys (rk_test_/rk_live_, per-resource None/Read/Write) are dashboard-created only with no public enumeration of permission categories, so the restricted-runtime-key story needs live-acceptance qualification rather than documentation citation. Compared with module-saas-starter/scripts/setup/stripe.sh, the proposal is a strict improvement: the script enforces the live guard purely via a ^sk_test_ regex (which would also reject legitimate rk_test_ restricted keys), sends no Idempotency-Key, sets no api_version, stamps no metadata, effectively adopts existing endpoints by URL match, and projects the management key as the runtime key — all behaviors the proposal correctly replaces or gates. Overall: Stripe fits the proposed contract well; the two HIGH findings must be resolved before P2 but neither requires a protocol redesign because REPLACE and host-side credential classification are already representable.

#### sentry
##### Lane 14: Sentry reference design

The Sentry v0.1 section is factually the strongest-verified provider section in the proposal: every checkable vendor claim held against current official docs (auth tokens over legacy API keys, project:read as the minimum scope for both project retrieval and client-key listing, org:ci as the documented CI/source-map/release scope, us.sentry.io/de.sentry.io regional origins, API v0, per-caller+endpoint rate limits with X-Sentry-Rate-Limit-* concurrency headers). Deferring project creation is correct, though the stated rationale is stale: the current endpoint POST /api/0/organizations/{org}/projects/ no longer requires a team and instead auto-creates a 'personal team' side effect, which is itself a good reason to defer. However, Sentry exposes two real design gaps the proposal has not closed. First, Sentry is the 'required design case' for regional/self-hosted origins (provider-plugins.md:1926) yet the broker/manifest model only supports an exact, package-time origin allowlist (provider-plugins.md:650,707-708,1088) — there is no specified mechanism for admitting a binding-supplied origin, and this must be fixed in the P1 manifest/credential-handle schema, not in P3. Second, the DSN classification story is internally contradictory (public browser file at :951, secret env file at :957, redacted-as-sensitive at :1523-1525) and classification authority rests on provider-declared outputs rather than the host-owned error-tracking@1 contract, which is exactly the over-/under-classification failure of scenario 18; the under-classification side is sharpened by the fact that the official client-keys list response carries legacy secret material (key `secret`, `dsn.secret`) one JSON pointer away from `dsn.public`. On the brief's closing question: Sentry only half-validates the abstraction. It genuinely pressures observation, projection, browser classification, multi-origin, and three-way credential-purpose separation (and the current script's conflation of setup and build token at sentry.sh:161/203 proves the purpose-separation feature is needed), but it exercises zero mutation machinery — no Apply, no idempotency, no checkpoints, no UNCERTAIN outcomes, no one-time capture, no destroy, no secret sink. Both mutation-bearing providers (Stripe, Resend) are webhook-endpoint CRUD, so invariant 35's claim that the protocol is 'designed against all three providers before being declared stable' is true for the read/project half and false for the mutation half. Sentry should stay second, but its acceptance criteria must be re-scoped to the things it can actually pressure (origin policy, contract-pinned classification, multi-key ambiguity, keys-response secret hygiene), and the protocol-freeze claim must be honest that mutation semantics are validated only against webhook-shaped resources.

#### resend
##### Lane 15: Resend reference design

The Resend v0.1 section (core/docs/provider-plugins.md:1735-1815) is substantially accurate against current official docs and is the best-verified of the three vendor sections: full_access vs sending_access exists exactly as claimed, domain_id restriction applies only to sending_access keys, webhooks are Svix-signed (svix-id/svix-timestamp/svix-signature) with documented at-least-once and out-of-order delivery, the default limit is 10 req/s per team with 429 plus IETF draft-06 ratelimit-* and retry-after headers, domain verification is a hard sending prerequisite with seven documented statuses, and the signing-secret documentation ambiguity the proposal flags is real. However the section under-specifies four things the current docs make unavoidable: (1) per current examples the signing secret is returned on plain GET retrieve, so secret-bearing responses occur on the read-only Observe/Doctor path, which the proposal's mutation-framed one-time capture mechanism (provider-plugins.md:495, 516) does not cover; (2) the capture mechanism is fail-open on shape variance — a moved/renamed signing_secret field silently misses the declared JSON Pointer and the raw whsec_ value flows to the provider process; (3) webhook listing is cursor-paginated and POST /webhooks has no idempotency support (idempotency keys cover only POST /emails and /emails/batch), so uniqueness determination and lost-response recovery both require a pagination contract the protocol lacks; (4) Resend provides no API-key introspection (list-api-keys returns neither permission nor domain_id), so the acceptance criterion 'management and sending-only scopes are distinguished' is satisfiable only by behavioral probing, which the credential-purpose model does not currently authorize, and the reverse error (full_access bound as runtime) is remotely undetectable. The proposal correctly identifies that the existing script's 'secret cannot be retrieved' assumption is stale, correctly demands live qualification instead of assumption, and correctly separates management/runtime credentials that the current script conflates (resend.sh:230 projects the management key as the runtime key). The section needs an explicit secret-retrievability capability/result model, a pagination/observation-completeness contract, and a defined local-forwarded secret story (the Resend CLI's tunnel and temp-webhook-secret mechanics are ambiguous between the docs page and the CLI 2.0 announcement) before P4 acceptance criteria are testable. None of this rejects Resend as the third provider — it is a genuinely useful counterexample — but two of the gaps (capture-miss fail-open, read-path secrets) are protocol-level and must land in the P1 foundation, not in P4.

#### reliability-ux-doctor
##### Lane 16: error model, rate limits, retries, reliability

The taxonomy reuse claim is accurate: core/proto/codefly/base/v0/failure.proto:12-175 already provides neutral FailureCode values (UNAUTHENTICATED=8, PERMISSION_DENIED=9, NOT_FOUND=4, RATE_LIMITED=18, TIMEOUT=16), a `retryable` bool (:158-159) and `retry_after` (:169-170), and executionreceipt supports UNCERTAIN (core/proto/codefly/execution/v1/execution.proto:22-23, core/executionreceipt/receipt.go:224) — REUSE AS-IS for the envelope. The reliability design around it, however, is materially under-specified: no single layer owns the retry loop (CLI coordinator, broker, and provider agent are all plausible readers of `retryable=true`), the taxonomy has no delivery-status dimension distinguishing 'request never sent' from 'sent, outcome unknown', and the proposal's own MaxUses=1 scoped authorization for mutations conflicts with its 'stable operation ID across transport retries' promise — a legitimate idempotency-keyed retry either exhausts the token or MaxUses is meaningless. Rate-limit handling is split between broker (header normalization) and provider (error translation) without an authority rule, so a provider that misclassifies a 429 or a post-send timeout as retryable can drive a generic retry layer into duplicate mutations, exactly what the brief forbids. Pagination completeness is unmodeled: Observe reports present/missing/inaccessible/ambiguous but has no 'enumeration truncated' state, and the broker's mandated smaller-than-4MiB body caps (toolbox baseline: toolbox-web/server.go:21) make truncation more likely, turning 'missing' into a duplicate CREATE. Offline behavior for Validate/Plan/UpgradeState is credibly enforced by sandbox+broker design and Tier-2 conformance ('offline RPCs truly have no network'), which is sound. Verdict: the error envelope reuse is real; the retry/ambiguity/ownership semantics must be specified in P1 before any provider is written (findings 1, 5).

##### Lane 17: CLI and operator experience

The 13-command surface is larger than needed: `show` duplicates `list BINDING`, `schema` is a flag's worth of behavior, `add` is subsumed by first-run `setup`, and `validate`/`observe` are internal phases of `plan` that CI can select with flags — a 8-command surface (in 'special') preserves every automation and debugging path while keeping `disconnect` and `destroy` as distinct destructive verbs per invariant 23. The founder path (`setup` with its 10 ordered steps) is well shaped and mirrors what the scripts proved. The serious gaps are contractual, not cosmetic: (a) exit codes are entirely unspecified while the existing doctor already commits to a 0/1 contract (cli/cmd/doctor_workspace.go:690-692) — CI cannot distinguish no-diff, diff-present, denied, approval-required, UNCERTAIN, or partial without one; (b) plan-file portability is undefined — the plan digest binds prior-state digest and state lives under ~/.codefly/providers/<canonical-workspace-path-sha256>/, so a plan produced on one CI runner can never apply on another, and the proposal offers `--out PLAN`/`apply --plan PLAN` without saying so; (c) approval UX is undefined for the two contexts that matter: a solo founder locally (who is the grantor?) and CI (how is a plan-digest-bound approval issued and consumed non-interactively?); (d) plan files have no stated secret-shape scan even though provider-authored 'safe canonical fields' are unverifiable by the host. Dry-run exists implicitly (`plan` is the dry run) but `setup --dry-run` should be explicit. Findings 6, 7, 8, 11.

##### Lane 18: doctor, observability, metrics, supportability

The claimed doctor foundation genuinely exists and is high quality: cli/cmd/doctor_workspace.go is strictly read-only with an explicit documented contract ('must not create, modify, or delete any file, and must not start agents, containers, or services', :92-95, :684-686), stable diagnostic codes with a breaking-change rule (:24-27), versioned JSON (:46), a 30s bound (:757), and a real-provider qualification record (cli/docs/doctor-workspace-qualification.md). But the proposal's plan to fold 12 provider sections including remote vendor calls into `codefly doctor workspace` contradicts that contract on three axes: it must start provider agents, it adds unbounded remote latency to a 30s local command, and it consumes shared vendor rate budgets — Resend's documented limit is 10 requests/second per team across all keys with 429 (https://resend.com/docs/api-reference/introduction), so a CI doctor loop throttles concurrent real operations. Doctor mutation-safety is asserted (security control 9) but the only per-request broker check the proposal specifies re-validates against 'the approved plan', which Doctor does not have — the operation-kind→HTTP-method ceiling must be made normative (finding 2). The proposed diagnostic code list has no Codefly-policy-denied code (only vendor `provider.auth.rejected`), so scenario 40's operator cannot distinguish a missing role grant from a bad Stripe key, and the new `provider.*` codes collide with the existing doctor's `provider_*` codes which mean SECRET provider (doctor_workspace.go:37-40). Metrics: the existing PDPMetrics baseline is label-free counters (core/policy/observability.go:25-100) — safe — but P5 promises operation metrics with no label allowlist; binding names, workspace hashes, and remote IDs must be forbidden as labels. Receipt correlation IDs (operation/attempt/plan/receipt) are well designed; support-bundle safety relies on assertions about provider-authored free text with no host-side secret-shape scan (the scan is specified only for cassettes). Findings 2, 3, 4, 9, 10.

#### testing-migration-roadmap
##### Lane 19: testing and conformance

The five-tier design (pure contract, recorded cassettes, provider conformance, opt-in live acceptance, starter dogfood) is structurally correct and its permission negative matrix (provider-plugins.md:583-600) is one of the strongest parts of the proposal. However, the two load-bearing test infrastructures are misrepresented as extensions of existing tooling. (1) 'codefly agent ci' conformance is hardcoded to kind codefly:service (cli/cmd/agents/ci.go:427-428; codefly:module already requires --skip-conformance, ci.go:422-425) and its conformance model is 'generate a service via Builder.Create, then run the workspace CI gate' (ci.go:554-579, 662-694) — none of which applies to a leaf-protocol provider agent; only the manifest/source/build/audit/drift stages and the GOWORK=off isolation pattern (ci.go:476-509) are reusable. Provider conformance is a new harness. (2) No cassette recorder/replayer/sanitizer exists anywhere in core/, cli/, or toolbox-web/ Go code — the pattern exists only as documentation citing the external mind-server repo (core/docs/testing.md:135-150). What 'NO MOCKS' should mean here: Tier 0 exercises pure functions with zero HTTP; Tier 1 replays recorded real-vendor bytes through the real production broker code path with enforcement active, failing hard (never falling back to live) on a cassette miss, with recording an explicit credentialed opt-in; hand-written fake Stripe/Sentry/Resend servers or in-process fake clients are never acceptance evidence; and the broker, PDP, sandbox, and sink in conformance tests must be the production implementations, never stubs. Symmetrically, default CI (every PR) must run only Tiers 0-2 so no merge depends on live vendor availability; Tier 3 runs scheduled/opt-in against dedicated accounts and is the only tier permitted to refresh cassettes, with every refresh a reviewed diff. Remaining gaps: the sanitizer's 'secret-shaped field remains' failure criterion is unspecified; deterministic replay matching versus broker-injected idempotency keys/timestamps is unaddressed; the neutral conformance provider's remote fixture is undefined; and the platform matrix (Linux CI + macOS) needed to actually prove sandbox network denial is not specified.

##### Lane 20: migration and compatibility

The migration design is directionally safe: no path in the proposal silently adopts or deletes a remote resource. URL-only matches become unmanaged conflicts requiring explicit endpoint-ID selection (provider-plugins.md:1961-1962, 1293-1295), deletion defaults to retain, disconnect is separated from destroy, and the projection writer refuses to overwrite unowned differing configuration (writer invariant 7). This matters because the current scripts do exactly what the proposal forbids: stripe.sh matches an existing webhook by URL+enabled status (module-saas-starter/scripts/setup/stripe.sh:168-176), persists no remote ID or state anywhere, and projects the management sk_test_ key as the runtime STRIPE_API_KEY (stripe.sh:215-226). Consequences the proposal underspecifies: (a) adoption step 4 'propose an import plan for exact remote IDs' has no source for those IDs since scripts never recorded them — Observe must present same-URL candidates with their IDs for operator selection, and behavior with multiple same-URL endpoints is undefined; (b) ownership metadata location is unspecified — if stored beside configuration files, the still-live legacy scripts (setup_install_pair, provider-common.sh:146-175, which installs files directly with --force override) can clobber it during the coexistence window; it must live in provider state; (c) the script-shim is not behavior-preserving — --provision-webhook maps to a binding-level lifecycle change, --skip-remote-validation has no equivalent (setup always observes), --force is replaced by plan/adoption — the proposal must declare which flags hard-fail with guidance; the four non-migrated scripts (workos/posthog/otel/turnstile) keep sourcing provider-common.sh, so shims must not break shared helpers; (d) old CLIs silently drop the new provider-bindings environment field because all resource YAML goes through permissive yaml.v3 Unmarshal (core/resources/marshalling.go:62-69) — no error, no diagnostic, just a missing capability; a minimum-CLI-version signal is needed. Rollback to scripts is possible without remote-state loss but leaves provider state claiming ownership of files the script rewrote — a diagnosed conflict, which is the correct failure mode.

##### Lane 21: production readiness

The proposal defines exactly two readiness gates — 'complete for local dogfood' and 'complete for production' (provider-plugins.md:2209-2240) — while separately permitting read-only production operation before a writable sink exists ('read-only production providers may run; production providers may project already existing secret references', provider-plugins.md:1010-1015). That combination creates an unnamed third state, 'production observe', with no mandatory feature list, which the brief explicitly requires and rejects collapsing. Production observe is not low-stakes: it spends a real management credential against a live account and must therefore require validated non-expired principals, scoped-auth-v2-only admission, artifact-digest-verified agents, proven fail-closed PDP, and receipt retention — none of which the local-dogfood gate requires. The gap is sharpened by wiring status: every policy composition primitive the gates depend on exists in core (WithPrincipal, WithScopedAuthSecret, WithPermissionsCallback, WithProductionAdmission at core/agents/manager/loader.go:485, 546, 589, 603) but has zero call sites in the CLI (rg across cli/ -g '*.go' returns none), so every gate item is new integration work, not existing production behavior. Reusable substance does exist for the evidence layer: Ed25519-signed receipts (core/executionreceipt/receipt.go:26, 85-131) and a bbolt journal with UNCERTAIN terminal semantics (cli/pkg/executionjournal/journal.go:22, 432, 796-808) are implemented and CLI-wired via the execution runtime/dispatcher (cli/cmd/daemon_execution.go). P5 additionally bundles the entire Gate-C infrastructure (remote state, writable sink, digest lock, DR) with GA operability/documentation, making the single 'production' milestone both vague and unreviewably broad. Required: the explicit three-gate model in the 'special' artifact, with Gate B added to the Definition of done before any production observe is enabled.

##### Lane 22: roadmap and GitHub issue decomposition

The dependency logic of the roadmap is sound — foundation before providers, Stripe freezes protocol v0, Sentry and Resend then proceed in parallel, hardening research overlaps — and the tension with invariant 35 ('designed against all three before declared stable') is resolved acceptably because the cross-provider matrix (provider-plugins.md:1908-1931) withholds 'stable' until all three pass. But the five-issue structure is not executable as written. Issue 1 spans two repositories (core, cli) and at least eight separable deliverable families — proto/kind/routing, the security-critical HTTP broker + credential handles + one-time capture, the coordinator/state/projection/CLI/doctor stack, and the conformance+cassette test infrastructure — far too large for safe review, especially when the broker/capture surface is the component most in need of focused adversarial review. Issue 5 bundles Gate-C production infrastructure with GA documentation. The structure also names no owning repositories, omits the three new provider repos, omits the module-saas-starter migration work hiding inside issues 2-4, and omits operational prerequisites (dedicated Stripe sandbox, Sentry test org, Resend account with a DNS-verified domain — a lead-time item) that gate the acceptance evidence of issues 2-4. Most important structural gap: the generic output contracts (codefly.dev/configuration/billing@1, email@1, error-tracking@1) referenced by bindings, manifests, projections, and conformance exist nowhere in code (rg for 'output-contract|codefly.dev/configuration' over core/ and cli/ returns nothing; the starter has only informal env-file conventions, e.g. setup_scripts_test.go:180-190) and no roadmap deliverable creates or assigns ownership of them — yet they are the contract that must freeze before P2-P4 can run in parallel. Replacement: eight issues (see 'special'), preserving the same serial spine while making the broker separately reviewable and adding the contract-registry deliverable to the foundation.

#### contradiction-audit
##### Lane 24: contradiction and completeness audit

The proposal is unusually internally consistent for its size (2423 lines), and every load-bearing 'Codefly already has this' claim I spot-checked against source is real: ScopedAuthorization with AudienceID/CatalogDigest/RequestDigest/MaxUses/Caveats (core/policy/scoped_auth.go:79-99, scoped_auth_v2.go:118-122), the manager options WithPrincipal/WithPermissionsCallback/WithProductionAdmission/WithScopedAuthSecret/WithoutPrincipal/WithoutSandbox (core/agents/manager/loader.go:453-603), strict empty-manifest deny in NewCeilingPDP (core/policy/pdp_ceiling.go:48-66), UNCERTAIN stage and Ed25519-signed receipts (core/executionreceipt/receipt.go:224, receipt_test.go:5-21), the read-only op:// 1Password resolver (core/configurations/secrets.go:32,124-132), the absence of any configuration writer (no non-test Write path in core/configurations — the 'loader is read-only' claim is accurate), toolbox-web allowlist/redirect-revalidation/timeout/body-cap guards (toolbox-web/server.go:17,23-26,33-98,251), `codefly agent ci` (cli/cmd/agents/ci.go:47), and sha256 workspace isolation (cli/pkg/executionruntime/runtime.go:61-71). TOOLBOX=5 is the current max agent kind (core/proto/codefly/base/v0/agent.proto:26), so the brief's 'PROVIDER=6' is the correct next value. However, the audit found three HIGH-severity internal contradictions that sit exactly on the protocol/trust surfaces P1 must freeze: (1) a manifest-gated escape hatch that lets raw secret values transit the provider process (provider-plugins.md:866-868) directly contradicting the absolute 'do not transit the provider process' / 'never sees the captured bytes' claims (486-488, 528-529) and brief invariant 14; (2) configuration-projection timing is specified three mutually inconsistent ways — as a PROJECT_OUTPUT plan action (1122), as a host 'Commit' authorization moment (per-operation authorization step 4), and as a post-Apply setup step 8 (1494-1495) matching the brief's 'Apply -> Project' stage — leaving undefined which mechanism projection-only providers (Sentry) use; (3) the secret-sink Prepare/Put/Commit/Rollback transaction contract (999-1004) is irreconcilable as written with the one-time-capture rules requiring immediate durable persistence at Put and forbidding rollback of a persisted one-time secret (520-525, 534-538). Three MEDIUM contradictions concern adopted-resource deletion semantics, the Sentry DSN being simultaneously public and secret in the same projection example, and idempotency-key ownership assigned to both broker and provider. One MEDIUM completeness gap: P2/Tier-4 acceptance requires webhook lifecycle in CI while the architecture explicitly declines to own the forwarder and never addresses CI ingress. One factual staleness: the claim that services.Advertisement 'assumes Builder and Runtime' ignores the existing CapabilityOnly mode (core/agents/services/advertisement.go:24). Brief-vs-proposal drift is minor: the brief hardens the products/prices non-goal into an unconditional prohibition the proposal only conditions, and the brief's linear 'Apply -> Project' lifecycle papers over the proposal's projection-timing ambiguity. None of the contradictions invalidates the overall architecture; all are resolvable by choosing one of the two stated behaviors, but the three HIGH items each pin a contract (input-classification schema, projection ownership, sink interface) that P1 freezes, so they must be resolved at P0/P1. I did not verify vendor-doc claims (the Resend signing-secret inconsistency at provider-plugins.md:84-87/1770-1779 is asserted, not verified by me — that check belongs to the provider lanes); no other Lane 24 category (permission-name mismatch between prose and manifest examples, resource-syntax mismatch, roadmap deliverables absent from architecture, state-required-but-never-persisted) produced a material finding beyond the LOW items reported: the manifest example's actions/resources (provider.observe/create/update/delete on provider:stripe/*) are consistent with the canonical vocabulary (395-403) and resource syntax (413-417), and every P1-P5 roadmap deliverable maps to an architecture section.

### Areas not verified, and why

- **No vendor resource was created, modified, or deleted, and no live API call was made with real credentials.** Every vendor claim rests on current official documentation. Consequently, three classes of behavior remain qualified only by documentation and are flagged in the report as requiring live acceptance: Stripe restricted-key permission categories (dashboard-created, not publicly enumerated), Resend webhook signing-secret retrievability across create/retrieve/list (vendor documentation is internally inconsistent), and Resend API-key introspection (the list endpoint returns neither permission nor domain restriction, so credential-scope confusion is remotely undetectable).
- **No sandbox escape was attempted.** The macOS and Linux isolation conclusions were derived by reading the generated Seatbelt profile and `bwrap` argv construction, not by running a hostile binary. The specific claims about `/proc/<pid>/environ` readability under the Linux profile and about macOS `mach-lookup` covert channels are labelled as inferences at MEDIUM and LOW confidence respectively and are the two places where a bounded experiment would change the report.
- **No build, code generation, or test suite was run.** The review is read-only by instruction; the brief's optional experiments (compiling policy tests, replaying toolbox redirect tests, inspecting a live agent launch environment) were not exercised. Claims about wiring status were established by exhaustive `rg` call-site searches rather than by running the code, which is sufficient to prove absence of call sites but not to prove behavior at runtime.
- **The ordinary `module-saas-starter/` worktree was not used as the starter reference**, per the brief's instruction to treat the six-integrations worktree as authoritative. One incidental citation to `module-saas-starter/scripts/setup/` appears in the testing-prerequisite table (section 11, Table C); the claim it supports — that no CI vendor-account provisioning or cleanup harness exists — holds in both worktrees. Neither worktree was modified.
- **AWS/Cloudflare request-signing documentation was not fetched.** The Lane 23 finding that request-signing credential schemes break the static header-placement handle model rests on the proposal's own internal contradiction (signature algorithms in the provider versus credential bytes in the broker), which is proven textually; the vendor-specific half is an inference at MEDIUM confidence.
- **No independent verification of MEDIUM and LOW findings.** The verification budget covered all reported BLOCKERs and the first fourteen HIGH findings. The remaining HIGH findings are labelled unverified in section 4; MEDIUM and LOW findings carry only the finding reviewer's own confidence assessment.
